Risk specification at the population level.

Our framework accommodates any convex risk measure satisfying
translation invariance, applied at any of four structurally distinct
levels of the IPO loss: the prompt distribution (
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{X}}
),
the response sampling distribution (
\mathcal{R}^{\rho}
), the
group/annotator/severity distribution (
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}
), and the
opponent distribution (
\mathcal{R}^{\mu}
). This decomposition already
captures sub-population heterogeneity, annotator disagreement, and
context-dependent risk aversion as design choices. Two extensions
go beyond what the current framework handles.

The first concerns
biased preference sources
. Practical
preference pipelines increasingly rely on AI judges, automated
annotators, or distilled reward models alongside human raters, each
of which introduces its own systematic biases and failure modes —
biases that compound when judges themselves are trained on human
data and inherit the same coverage gaps. Modeling such sources as
additional components of the
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}
stratification is
straightforward in principle, but quantifying
how
judge bias
propagates through the risk-adjusted equilibrium, and whether
risk-sensitivity at the policy level can correct for systematic
miscalibration at the judge level, is an open question with
significant practical stakes.

The second is
(multiplayer) performative prediction

(
44
;
42
)
.
Our analysis treats the latent distribution
\Omega
over which the
risk operates—annotators, sub-populations, contexts—as
exogenous to the players’ actions. In deployment this is rarely
true: the population of users a model interacts with, the prompts it
encounters, and the annotator pool that evaluates it all shift in
response to the policy itself. Performative effects of this kind
break the i.i.d. assumption underlying our sample complexity
analysis and turn the risk-adjusted equilibrium into a fixed point of
a coupled system:
\Omega
is now a function of the joint policy

(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, not an independent draw. Extending the
risk-sensitive framework to this setting—e.g., where the risk measure
itself depends on the actions over which we are optimizing—is a
substantively different problem, and one that we view as the most
important applied direction beyond this work as users respond to these models and the models are typically adapting in some way on the fly.

Empirical scope.

Our empirical results establish that risk adjustment provides
distributional control on held-out severity strata at the scale of
our experiments. Three directions extend this scope. First,
scaling: how do the constants in our generalization bounds behave at
modern frontier scale, and does the variance floor become the
binding constraint? Second, deployment-time guarantees: turning
risk-adjusted equilibria into auditable safety properties on
specified subpopulations, rather than only on the strata used at
training time. Third, alignment to populations rather than
representative users: a normative as well as a technical project,
requiring practitioners to specify which tails matter and to what
degree.

Our experiments are on safety alignment on PKU-SafeRLHF with LoRA
fine-tuning; the qualitative findings should generalize to other
alignment domains and scales, but quantitative effects and optimal
risk parameters are likely domain-dependent.

Choosing the Risk Measure.

Finally, the framework gives tools for optimizing against a chosen
risk measure but is silent on
which
risk measure to choose.
Selecting the right risk measure for a given alignment objective is
a modeling question we view as the natural next step rather than a
gap in the present work.

Appendix C
Overview of Game-Theoretic Algorithms

In this section we provide an overview of reinforcement learning from human feedback and relevant game-theoretic algorithms for preference learning from human feedback.

C.1
Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback

Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF)
(
14
;
74
)
fits a scalar reward model from pairwise comparison data and then optimizes a policy against it. Given a dataset
\{(x^{(i)},y_{w}^{(i)},y_{l}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{N}
where
y_{w}
is preferred to
y_{l}
conditional on prompt
x
, RLHF assumes a Bradley-Terry model
(
7
)

\mathbb{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)=\sigma\left(r_{\phi}(x,y)-r_{\phi}(x,y^{\prime})\right),\qquad\sigma(t)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-t}},

(14)

and learns the reward model
r_{\phi}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
by minimizing the cross-entropy loss

\mathcal{L}_{r}(\phi)=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\sigma\left(r_{\phi}(x^{(i)},y_{w}^{(i)})-r_{\phi}(x^{(i)},y_{l}^{(i)})\right).

(15)

The policy is then optimized against this reward subject to a KL penalty toward a reference policy
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
,

\pi^{\star}_{\phi}=\argmax_{\pi}\;\E_{x\sim\rho_{\mathcal{X}}}\left[\E_{y\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{\phi}(x,y)\right]-\beta\,\KL(\pi(\cdot\mid x)\|\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(\cdot\mid x))\right],

(16)

typically using PPO
(
55
)
or a variant such as GRPO
(
57
)
. Under tabular softmax parameterization, (
16
) admits the closed-form solution

\theta^{\star}_{\phi}=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{r_{\phi}}{\beta},

(17)

which DPO
(
45
)
exploits to optimize the policy directly from preference pairs without explicit reward modeling. The reliance on (
14
) restricts RLHF to preference structures expressible by a scalar reward and transitive preferences, motivating the preference-game formulation of NLHF.

C.2
Nash Learning from Human Feedback

Nash learning from human feedback
(
41
)
replaces the scalar reward model of RLHF with a preference model
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\to[0,1]
that takes both responses as input

\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)=\mathbb{P}\bigl[\text{a randomly chosen annotator prefers $y$ to $y^{\prime}$ given $x$}\bigr],

(18)

and satisfies
\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)+\mathcal{P}(y^{\prime}\succ y\mid x)=1
. Following the setup in Section
2
, we suppress the prompt
x
and assume
|\mathcal{X}|=1
for notational simplicity; all definitions extend by conditioning on the prompt. Collecting the preference probabilities into a matrix
P=[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime})]_{(y,y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, the anti-symmetry of
\mathcal{P}
becomes the constant-sum identity

P+P^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}.

(19)

For two policies,
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
, the preference of
\pi_{1}
over
\pi_{2}
is

V(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=\mathcal{P}(\pi_{1}\succ\pi_{2})=\E_{y\sim\pi_{1},\,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{2}}\left[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime})\right]=\pi_{1}^{\top}P\,\pi_{2},

(20)

which together with (
19
) defines a two-player constant-sum matrix game. The NLHF objective is to find a policy that wins on average against any opponent,

\pi^{\star}=\argmax_{\pi}\,\min_{\pi^{\prime}}\,V(\pi,\pi^{\prime}),

(21)

which is a Nash equilibrium of the preference game by the minimax theorem
(
64
)
.

Regularized game and the QRE.

As in RLHF, NLHF in practice regularizes toward a reference policy
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}\in\Delta^{\circ}(\mathcal{Y})
via a KL penalty. The KL-regularized value function and corresponding objective are

\displaystyle V_{\beta}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}^{\top}P\,\pi_{2}-\beta\,\KL(\pi_{1}\|\pi_{\texttt{ref}})+\beta\,\KL(\pi_{2}\|\pi_{\texttt{ref}}),

(22)

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\pi_{1}}\,\min_{\pi_{2}}\,V_{\beta}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}).

(23)

The KL term makes
V_{\beta}
strictly concave in
\pi_{1}
and strictly convex in
\pi_{2}
, so the regularized game has a unique equilibrium
(
41
)
. Under a softmax parameterization
\pi_{\theta}\propto\exp(\theta)
, the first-order optimality condition for each player produces a coupled pair of fixed-point equations

\theta_{1}^{\star}=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{P\,\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\star}}}{\beta},\qquad\theta_{2}^{\star}=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{P^{\top}\,\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\star}}}{\beta},

(24)

which the constant-sum identity (
19
) collapses to a single equation

\theta^{\star}_{\beta}=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{P\,\pi^{\star}_{\beta}}{\beta},

(25)

with
\pi_{1}^{\star}=\pi_{2}^{\star}=\pi^{\star}_{\beta}
at equilibrium. The unique solution to (
25
) is the
quantal response equilibrium
(QRE) of the regularized preference game
(
40
)
. Every game-theoretic NLHF algorithm we discuss below targets (
25
) at its fixed point.

C.3
The IPO loss as a unifying lens

Solving the fixed-point equation (
25
) requires a tractable loss whose gradient drives the iterates toward the QRE. The generalized IPO loss
(
3
;
9
;
72
)
provides exactly this. Let
\rho\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y})
be a sampling distribution over response pairs and
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
be an opponent distribution. The
generalized IPO loss
is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}(\theta;\rho,\mu)=\E_{(y,y^{\prime})\sim\rho}\left[\left(\log\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y)\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime})}{\pi_{\theta}(y^{\prime})\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)}-\frac{1}{\beta}\,\E_{y^{\prime\prime}\sim\mu}\left[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})-\mathcal{P}(y^{\prime}\succ y^{\prime\prime})\right]\right)^{2}\right].

(26)

Define the response-pair preconditioner

\Sigma(\rho):=\E_{(y,y^{\prime})\sim\rho}\left[(\mathbf{1}_{y}-\mathbf{1}_{y^{\prime}})(\mathbf{1}_{y}-\mathbf{1}_{y^{\prime}})^{\top}\right].

(27)

A direct calculation gives the gradient identity

\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}(\theta;\rho,\mu)=2\,\Sigma(\rho)\left(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}-\frac{P\mu}{\beta}\right).

(28)

Two structural observations follow. First, the bracket in (
28
) is exactly the residual of the QRE fixed-point equation (
25
) when the opponent plays
\mu
, so the IPO gradient is a preconditioned fixed-point residual. Second,
\rho
and
\mu
play distinct structural roles:
\rho
shapes the geometry of the update through
\Sigma(\rho)
but does not alter the equilibrium, while
\mu
is the opponent against which the preference operator
P
is probed and is where the game-theoretic content lives.

This decomposition organizes the game-theoretic NLHF literature. Every algorithm we discuss below is determined by three choices: a sampling distribution
\rho
, an opponent
\mu
, and a step rule (a single forward step, a magnetic-adjusted forward step, or a two-step extragradient update) applied to the gradient (
28
). The algorithms differ in these choices but share (
25
) as their target fixed point.

C.4
Algorithms in the
(\rho,\mu)
framework

Throughout this subsection
\bar{\pi}:=\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{Y})\otimes\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{Y})
denotes uniform sampling over response pairs,
\mathrm{sg}[\cdot]
denotes the stop-gradient operator, and
\eta>0
is a step size. All algorithms perform parameter updates on
\theta
using the IPO gradient (
28
) evaluated at specific choices of
(\rho,\mu)
.

Online IPO/OMD.

The online IPO algorithm of
9
sets
\rho=\bar{\pi}
and
\mu=\mathrm{sg}[\pi^{(t)}]
. The update is a single gradient step on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}(\theta;\bar{\pi},\mathrm{sg}[\pi^{(t)}])
, equivalent to the online mirror descent recursion

\theta^{(t+1)}=(1-\eta\beta)\,\theta^{(t)}+\eta\beta\left(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{P\,\pi^{(t)}}{\beta}\right).

(29)

This is Picard iteration on (
25
) with the opponent identified with the player’s current policy. Convergence is in average iterate rate
\mathcal{O}(1/T)
; the last iterate cycles around the QRE and is not guaranteed to converge.

Nash-MD.

41
introduce Nash-MD with
\rho=\bar{\pi}
and
\mu=\mathrm{sg}[\pi^{(t)}_{\mathrm{mix}}]
, where
\pi^{(t)}_{\mathrm{mix}}
is the geometric mixture

\pi^{(t)}_{\mathrm{mix}}(y)\propto\pi^{(t)}(y)^{1-\eta\beta}\,\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)^{\eta\beta}.

(30)

The opponent is pulled toward
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
, which acts as a magnet that breaks the cycling of vanilla OMD. The authors establish last-iterate convergence to the QRE in KL divergence rate
\mathcal{O}(1/T)
with step size
\eta_{t}=\Theta(1/(\beta t))
.

Nash-MD-PG.

41
also proposes a policy-gradient counterpart of Nash-MD, targeting the same fixed point but implemented through nested inner optimization rather than the single-step IPO update. Each outer iteration approximately solves an inner regularized best-response problem against
\pi^{(t)}_{\mathrm{mix}}
via several gradient steps. The approximation introduces inner-loop error that accumulates across outer iterations, convergence guarantees in the small-
\beta
regime are not available.

MPO.

65
replace the geometric-mixture magnet of Nash-MD with a slowly-moving anchor:
\mu=\mathrm{sg}[\pi^{(\tau(t))}]
for a lagged index
\tau(t)<t
, with the anchor refreshed on a slower timescale. They show last-iterate linear convergence to the QRE at rate
(1+\eta\beta)^{-T}
with step size constraint
\eta\leq\mathcal{O}(\beta)
.

EGPO.

72
introduce a two-step extragradient update keeping
\rho=\bar{\pi}
throughout but alternating
\mu
. The extrapolation step uses
\mu=\mathrm{sg}[\pi^{(t)}]
to produce an intermediate iterate, and the correction step uses
\mu=\pi^{(t+1/2)}
as the opponent:

\displaystyle\theta^{(t+1/2)}

\displaystyle=(1-\eta\beta)\,\theta^{(t)}+\eta\beta\left(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{P\,\pi^{(t)}}{\beta}\right),

(31)

\displaystyle\theta^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=(1-\eta\beta)\,\theta^{(t)}+\eta\beta\left(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{P\,\pi^{(t+1/2)}}{\beta}\right).

(32)

The correction step queries preferences against the predicted opponent rather than the current one, which removes the rotational component responsible for the cycling of OMD. The authors establish last-iterate linear convergence in KL divergence at rate
(1-\eta\beta)^{T}
for any step size
\eta\leq 1/(\beta+3)
, and show that under sub-Gaussian gradient noise with variance proxy
\sigma^{2}
, the rate is preserved with an additive
\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}\log|\mathcal{Y}|/\beta)
term.

Appendix D
Sources of Uncertainty in Preference Learning Data

The main paper develops risk over the opponent distribution
\mu
. Three other places where risk can enter are the prompt distribution, an auxiliary variable indexing within-prompt heterogeneity, and the response-pair sampler. We treat each in turn and then discuss composition of risks.

Setup.

Restoring the prompt
x\in\mathcal{X}
explicitly, the preference oracle returns
\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)
and may depend on an auxiliary variable
w\in\mathcal{W}
giving
\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x,w)
. The per-prompt risk-adjusted IPO loss with risk over
\mu
is

\ell_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\theta;x,\rho,\mu)=\E_{(y,y^{\prime})\sim\rho\mid x}\left[\left(\log\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y\mid x)\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime}\mid x)}{\pi_{\theta}(y^{\prime}\mid x)\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y\mid x)}-\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl((P_{\mathcal{R}}\,\mu)_{y}-(P_{\mathcal{R}}\,\mu)_{y^{\prime}}\bigr)\right)^{2}\right],

(33)

and the aggregate training loss takes the outer expectation over prompts,

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\theta;\rho,\mu)=\E_{x\sim\rho_{\mathcal{X}}}\left[\ell_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\theta;x,\rho,\mu)\right].

(34)

The remaining cases correspond to placing risk on
x
, on
w
, or on the response-pair sampler
\rho
.

D.1
Risk over the prompt distribution

Replace the outer expectation
\E_{x\sim\rho_{\mathcal{X}}}
in (
34
) with a risk functional
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{X}}
over prompts,

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{X}}}(\theta;\rho,\mu)=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{X}}\left[\ell_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\theta;x,\rho,\mu)\right].

(35)

This is distributionally robust optimization over prompts. Natural choices for
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{X}}
are CVaR at level
\alpha
, which focuses on the worst-
\alpha
tail of difficult prompts, and KL-ball DRO, which optimizes over the worst re-weighting of
\rho_{\mathcal{X}}
within a KL ball. The per-prompt fixed-point equation is unchanged; for each
x
, the equilibrium
\theta^{\star}_{\beta,\mathcal{R}}(\cdot\mid x)
still satisfies the QRE condition with operator
P_{\mathcal{R}}(\cdot,\cdot\mid x)
. What changes is the effective training distribution over prompts; the algorithm acts as if prompts were drawn from an adversarial re-weighting of
\rho_{\mathcal{X}}
. Risk over
x
does not alter the game; it alters which per-prompt game the algorithm prioritizes fitting across the population.

D.2
Risk over within-prompt heterogeneity

When the preference oracle depends on an auxiliary variable
w
indexing groups, annotators, or latent labels, the risk-neutral preference operator integrates over
w\mid x
,

\E_{w\mid x}\left[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}\mid x,w)\right].

(36)

Replacing the expectation with a risk functional
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}
defines the doubly risk-adjusted preference operator

(P_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}},\mathcal{R}^{\mu}}\,\mu)_{y}^{x}:=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}\left[\mathcal{R}^{\mu}\left[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}\mid x,w)\,:\,y^{\prime\prime}\sim\mu\right]\,:\,w\mid x\right],

(37)

which sits in the same position as
P_{\mathcal{R}}
inside the per-prompt loss (
33
). The per-prompt QRE fixed point becomes

\theta^{\star}_{\beta,\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}},\mathcal{R}^{\mu}}(\cdot\mid x)=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)+\frac{P_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}},\mathcal{R}^{\mu}}(\cdot,\cdot\mid x)\,\pi^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)}{\beta}.

(38)

Structurally, risk over
w
acts on the inner preference operator in the same way as risk over
\mu
—both replace an expectation in the preference computation with a risk functional and produce the same kind of fixed-point modification. The difference is what each models. Risk over
\mu
targets robustness against an adversarial opponent in the game, whereas risk over
w
targets robustness across heterogeneous evaluators of a fixed pair-wise comparison. When
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}
is CVaR over annotator identity or a worst-case over safety labels, this recovers a group-robust formulation closely related to group-DRO.

D.3
Risk over the response-pair sampler

Risk over the response pair sampler
\rho
replaces the outer expectation
\E_{(y,y^{\prime})\sim\rho}
in the per-prompt loss (
33
) with a risk functional. This modification reshapes the preconditioner that multiplies the gradient and changes the geometry of the update, but does not modify the bracket of the IPO gradient identity. The fixed-point equation is therefore unchanged, and the algorithm still targets the same risk-adjusted QRE, therefore risk over
\rho
acts purely as a sampling knob.

D.4
Composition of
\mathcal{R}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}

Risk over
\mu
and risk over
w
both modify the inner preference operator and the two can be composed. Risk functionals do not commute in general, so the order of composition is a modeling choice. The two natural orderings are

\displaystyle\text{(A)}\quad(P_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}\circ\mathcal{R}^{\mu}}\,\mu)_{y}^{x}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}_{w}\left[\mathcal{R}^{\mu}_{y^{\prime\prime}}\left[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}\mid x,w)\right]\right],

(39)

\displaystyle\text{(B)}\quad(P_{\mathcal{R}^{\mu}\circ\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}}\,\mu)_{y}^{x}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{\mu}_{y^{\prime\prime}}\left[\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}_{w}\left[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}\mid x,w)\right]\right].

(40)

Ordering (A) corresponds to applying the opponent-risk first and group-risk on the outside, meaning the policy is robust against an adversarial opponent within each group, and then robust across groups. Ordering (B) reverses this, the policy is robust across groups for each opponent draw, and then robust against an adversarial opponent. The two coincide when at least one of
\mathcal{R}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}
is an expectation, in which case the construction reduces to the risk-neutral preference operator. When both reduce to expectations, the operator is risk-neutral. The gradient identity

\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}^{\mu},\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}}(\theta;\rho,\mu)=2\,\Sigma(\rho)\left(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}-\frac{P_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{W}}\circ\mathcal{R}^{\mu}}\,\mu}{\beta}\right)

(41)

holds with either ordering substituted into the operator. The bracket remains the residual of the corresponding fixed-point equation, and the strong-monotonicity analysis of the main paper applies with the composed operator in place of
P_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Appendix E
Risk on Other Preference Losses

The risk modification developed in the main paper is not specific to IPO. The same construction—replace an expectation over preference outcomes with a convex risk functional—applies to the broader family of preference-tuning losses. We work through the construction for IPO
(
9
)
, DPO
(
45
)
, GRPO
(
57
)
, and KTO
(
18
)
. Each loss corresponds to a particular choice of risk functional
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
(parametrized by a risk level
\tau
) and reference distribution
\mu
, and the standard risk-neutral loss is recovered in the limit of
\tau
.

Throughout, we write
h_{\pi}(y,y^{\prime}):=\log\frac{\pi(y)\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime})}{\pi(y^{\prime})\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)}
for the log-ratio term,
X_{y}:=P(y\succ Y^{\prime})
for the random variable representing the win-rate of
y
against an opponent
Y^{\prime}\sim\mu
, and
R_{\tau}(y):=-\mathcal{R}_{\tau}(X_{y})
for the risk-adjusted action value. The squared-residual form

\widehat{L}_{\tau}(\pi)=\E_{(y,y^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(h_{\pi}(y,y^{\prime})-\beta^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{R}_{\tau,m}(y)-\widehat{R}_{\tau,m}(y^{\prime})\bigr)\right)^{2}\right]

(42)

is common to each loss; what changes is the choice of
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
,
\mu
, and the resulting estimator
\widehat{R}_{\tau,m}
.

E.1
IPO with entropic risk

Take
\mu
to be the data distribution
\mathcal{D}
over response pairs and
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
to be the entropic risk measure

\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\text{ent}}(X_{y})=\frac{1}{\tau}\log\E_{Y^{\prime}\sim\mu}\left[e^{-\tau P(y\succ Y^{\prime})}\right],

(43)

giving the risk-adjusted action value

R_{\tau}^{\text{IPO}}(y)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\log\E_{Y^{\prime}\sim\mu}\left[e^{-\tau P(y\succ Y^{\prime})}\right].

(44)

Given
Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mu
, the plug-in estimator is

\widehat{R}_{\tau,m}^{\text{IPO}}(y)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\log\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}e^{-\tau P(y\succ Y^{\prime}_{j})}\right),

(45)

and substituting into (
42
) yields the risk-adjusted IPO loss. As
\tau\to 0
, a Taylor expansion gives
\widehat{R}_{\tau,m}^{\text{IPO}}(y)\to\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j}P(y\succ Y^{\prime}_{j})
, the empirical mean win-rate, and the standard IPO loss is recovered.

E.2
GRPO with reverse-KL risk

GRPO
(
57
)
draws a group of opponent responses from the current policy and computes group-normalized advantages. The corresponding risk formulation takes
\mu=\pi_{\text{old}}
and uses the reverse-KL risk measure

\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\text{RKL}}(X_{y})=\sup_{p\in\Delta}\left[\mathbb{E}_{p}[-X_{y}]-\frac{1}{\tau}\KL(\mu\,\|\,p)\right].

(46)

The KKT optimality condition for the inner adversary gives

p^{\star}(y^{\prime\prime})=\frac{\mu(y^{\prime\prime})}{\tau(\mu_{0}-P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}))},

(47)

with
\mu_{0}>1
fixed by
\sum_{y^{\prime\prime}}p^{\star}(y^{\prime\prime})=1
. Substituting and applying the Fenchel identity for
\varphi(t)=-\log t
produces

R_{\tau}^{\text{RKL}}(y)=\mu_{0}-\frac{1}{\tau}\E_{y^{\prime\prime}\sim\mu}\left[1+\log\frac{1}{\tau(\mu_{0}-P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}))}\right].

(48)

The weights
p^{\star}(y^{\prime\prime})\propto\mu(y^{\prime\prime})/(\mu_{0}-P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}))
up-weight opponents against which
y
wins by a small margin—the hardest matchup in the group—producing a risk-averse analogue of group-relative importance weighting. The plug-in estimator
\widehat{R}_{\tau,m}^{\text{RKL}}
replaces the expectation over
y^{\prime\prime}\sim\mu
with the empirical mean over a group
Y^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}
and substitutes an empirical
\widehat{\mu}_{0}
for the normalization. As
\tau\to\infty
,
\mu_{0}\to 1^{+}
and
p^{\star}\to\mu
, so
R_{\tau}^{\text{RKL}}(y)\to\E_{y^{\prime\prime}\sim\mu}[P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})]
, recovering the empirical group win-rate that defines the standard GRPO advantage.

E.3
DPO with entropic risk and frozen opponent

DPO
(
45
)
corresponds to the best-response variant of the preference game, the opponent is frozen at
\mu=\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
rather than co-optimized. With the same entropic risk as IPO,

R_{\tau}^{\text{DPO}}(y)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\log\E_{Y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{ref}}}\left[e^{-\tau P(y\succ Y^{\prime})}\right],

(49)

and the plug-in estimator
\widehat{R}_{\tau,m}^{\text{DPO}}
is computed from
m
draws
Y^{\prime}_{j}\sim\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
. As
\tau\to 0
, the action value collapses to
\E_{Y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{ref}}}[P(y\succ Y^{\prime})]
. Under a Bradley-Terry model, this expected win rate against
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
is in the same equivalence class as the optimal reward
r^{\star}
, recovering the standard DPO objective up to the substitution of a squared residual for the cross-entropy form. The risk-adjusted version replaces
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
with the risk-tilted opponent
p^{\star}(y^{\prime\prime})\propto\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime\prime})e^{-\tau P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})}
.

E.4
KTO with utility-based shortfall risk

KTO
(
18
)
is built on the Kahneman-Tversky value function with a sigmoid utility, encoding loss aversion through asymmetric saturation. The pairwise-preference analogue uses the utility-based shortfall risk measure with sigmoid utility
u=\sigma
, risk tolerance
r>0
, and
\mu=\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
:

\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\text{sf}}(X_{y})=\inf_{p\in\Delta}\left[\mathbb{E}_{p}[-X_{y}]+\frac{1}{\tau}\inf_{\alpha>0}\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(r+\mathbb{E}_{p}\left[u^{*}\left(\alpha\frac{\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime\prime})}{p(y^{\prime\prime})}\right)\right]\right)\right].

(50)

The KKT condition for inner adversary yields

p^{\star}(y^{\prime\prime})\propto\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime\prime})\cdot\sigma\bigl(\tau(\mu_{0}-P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}))-r\bigr),

(51)

and the risk-adjusted action value is

R_{\tau}^{\text{KTO}}(y)=\E_{Y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{ref}}}\left[P(y\succ Y^{\prime})\cdot\sigma\bigl(\tau(\mu_{0}-P(y\succ Y^{\prime}))-r\bigr)\right]+C(\tau,\mu_{0},r),

(52)

where
C
is a constant in
y
. The sigmoid weights
\sigma(\tau(\mu_{0}-P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime}))-r)
implement the loss aversion: opponents against which the policy wins by a small margin—those near the reference point
r
—receive the highest weight, encoding the prospect-theoretic asymmetry that motivates the original KTO. As
\tau\to 0
, the sigmoid saturates to a constant and
R_{\tau}^{\text{KTO}}(y)
reduces to a scaled expected win-rate against
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
plus a
y
-independent constant, recovering the structural template of standard KTO under the squared-residual form. We note that the original KTO of
18
is defined on per-example desirability labels rather than pairwise preferences, thus the construction above is the natural pairwise-preference analogue, retaining the sigmoid utility and the loss-aversion structure of KTO while operating on the same preference oracle
P(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})
used by IPO, DPO, and GRPO.

Appendix F
Monotonicity of the Risk-Adjusted Operator

In the body, we demonstrated the conditions under which it was sufficient to consider a single player fixed point iteration (Theorem
1
), as many of our practical implementations followed that framework. However, there are many scenarios where those conditions are violated. This is because in general
(P_{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}\,\mu)_{y}+(P_{\mathcal{R}}^{(2)}\,\mu)_{y}\neq 1
, i.e., risk typically breaks the constant sum structure.

F.1
Translation Invariance Preserves Constant Sum Structure

The risk-neutral analysis of NLHF relies on the constant-sum identity

P+P^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
to collapse the coupled fixed-point
equations (
5
) into the single expression (
2
) satisfied
by both players at equilibrium. Under nonlinear risk transformations, this structure
no longer holds at the level of certainty equivalents: for a general convex risk
measure
\mathcal{R}
, one has
\mathcal{R}(X)\neq-\mathcal{R}(-X)
, so zero-sum structure is
broken at the utility level.
Indeed, as observed in Example
1
, at the certainty equivalent level (aka the utility level) risk transformations are nonlinear and therefore break the zero-sum structure of the preference learning problem (or any zero sum matrix game for that matter).

We first show that despite this apparent shortcoming, when the risk measure is translation invariant, as are convex risk measures, the property
\mathcal{R}(1-X)=1+\mathcal{R}(-X)
ensures the constant sum structure is preserved. We formally restate and prove Lemma
1
from the main.
See
1

Proof.

Fix
a\in\mathcal{Y}
and let
Y^{\prime}\sim\mu
. Since

P+P^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
, we have

P_{a,Y^{\prime}}=1-P_{Y^{\prime},a}.

Therefore, by translation invariance of
\mathcal{R}
, we have that

(P^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{a}=\mathcal{R}(P_{a,Y^{\prime}})=\mathcal{R}(1-P_{Y^{\prime},a})=1+\mathcal{R}(-P_{Y^{\prime},a})=1+(P^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{a}.

Since this holds for every
a\in\mathcal{Y}
, the vector identity follows.
∎

Gauge invariance.

The softmax map
\mathrm{softmax}(\theta):=\frac{e^{\theta}}{\mathbf{1}^{\top}e^{\theta}}

satisfies
\mathrm{softmax}(\theta+c\mathbf{1})=\mathrm{softmax}(\theta)

for every scalar
c
. Adding a constant to all entries of a logit
vector therefore leaves the policy unchanged; this is the
one-dimensional gauge of the parameterization.

Translation Invariant Risk Measures.

Many well known risk measures are translation invariant. In particular, the commonly used class of
convex
risk measures (Definition
1
). In the remainder of this section we prove this property for the two risk measures we use in experiments, entropic risk and CVaR.

Lemma 2

(Entropic risk obeys translation invariance)
.

Recall that the entropic risk of a random variable
Z
with risk parameter
\tau
is given by
\text{Ent}_{\tau}(Z)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\log\left(\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\tau Z)]\right)
. For any constant
c
we have
\text{Ent}_{\tau}(Z+c)=\text{Ent}_{\tau}(Z)+c
.

Proof.

The result is immediate from the following simple algebraic manipulations:

\displaystyle\text{Ent}_{\tau}(Z+c)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\tau}\log\left(\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\tau Z-\tau c)]\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\tau}\log\bigl(\exp(-\tau c)\,\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\tau Z)]\bigr)

\displaystyle=c-\frac{1}{\tau}\log\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\tau Z)]

\displaystyle=\text{Ent}_{\tau}(Z)+c.

∎

We now prove the same for CVaR. The mechanism is different: entropic risk-aversion factors a constant shift through the exponential, whereas CVaR uses translation invariance directly. Yet, the conclusion is identical.

For a real-valued random variable
Z
with distribution function
F_{Z}
,
define the lower
\alpha
-quantile
\mathrm{Var}_{\alpha}(Z):=\inf\{z:F_{Z}(z)\geq\alpha\}
and the lower-tail conditional expectation

\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z)\;:=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\,\E\bigl[Z\cdot\mathbf{1}\{Z\leq\mathrm{Var}_{\alpha}(Z)\}\bigr]\,+\,\text{atom-correction}\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\alpha}\mathrm{Var}_{u}(Z)\,\,\mathrm{d}u,

(53)

the second equality holding for general distributions

(
49
)
. Risk-averse use of CVaR takes the

lower
tail, with
\alpha\in(0,1)
the tail probability;

\alpha\to 1
recovers
\E[Z]
,
\alpha\to 0
recovers

\mathrm{essinf}(Z)
. The Rockafellar–Uryasev variational formula

\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z)\;=\;\sup_{\eta}\Bigl\{\eta-\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\,\E\bigl[(\eta-Z)_{+}\bigr]\Bigr\}

(54)

makes the translation property transparent: the supremum over
\eta

shifts by
c
when
Z
is replaced by
Z+c
.

Lemma 3

(Translation invariance of CVaR)
.

For any real-valued random variable
Z
on a probability space and any
deterministic constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z+c)\;=\;\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z)+c.

Proof.

By (
54
), the change of variable
\eta\mapsto\eta+c

gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z+c)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\eta}\bigl\{\eta-\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\E[(\eta-(Z+c))_{+}]\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\eta^{\prime}}\bigl\{(\eta^{\prime}+c)-\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\E[(\eta^{\prime}-Z)_{+}]\bigr\}

\displaystyle=c+\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z).

Equivalently,
\mathrm{Var}_{\alpha}(Z+c)=\mathrm{Var}_{\alpha}(Z)+c
(quantiles
shift by
c
), and (
53
) preserves this shift through the
conditional expectation.
∎

F.2
Symmetric Self-play is Enough

For the ease of reference, we recall Theorem
1
from the main body before proving it.
See
1

Proof.

Let
(\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\star}},\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\star}})
denote the unique Nash equilibrium, satisfying the coupled fixed-point equations (
5
). Since
\mathcal{R}
is translation invariant and
P+P^{\top}=\bf 11^{\top}
by Lemma
1
we have that

(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}\,\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\star}})_{a}=(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}^{1}\,\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\star}})_{a}-1.

Let
\widetilde{\theta}_{2}:=\theta_{2}^{\star}-\beta^{-1}\mathbf{1}
, and note
\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\star}}=\pi_{\widetilde{\theta}_{2}}
by gauge invariance. Substituting into (
5
) yields

\theta_{1}^{\star}=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}^{1}\,\pi_{\widetilde{\theta}_{2}}}{\beta},\qquad\widetilde{\theta}_{2}=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}^{1}\,\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\star}}}{\beta},

which are symmetric in
\theta_{1}^{\star}
and
\widetilde{\theta}_{2}
. Uniqueness of the equilibrium forces
\theta_{1}^{\star}=\widetilde{\theta}_{2}
, and hence
\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\star}}=\pi_{\widetilde{\theta}_{2}}=\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\star}}
.
∎

F.3
The two-player risk-adjusted game

In the specific case of a constant sum preference matrix and shared translation invariant risk measures, we have just shown that symmetric self play suffices. However, there are many settings under which it may be desirable to break one or more of those conditions. For example one might want to solve a preference game with
asymmetric
risk profiles, considering both a risk-averse and a risk-seeking player. In these settings, the game is genuinely general-sum and it no longer suffices to consider symmetric self play. The goal of this section is to study the monotonicity of risk-averse games in the most general setting, as this property is key to proving existence and convergence. We specialize these results to the symmetric self play operator in Section
F.4
.

Recall that each player
i\in\{1,2\}
chooses logits
\theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}

inducing a softmax policy
\pi_{\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
. The
risk-adjusted utility for player
i
is

V_{\beta}^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\;=\;\pi_{\theta_{i}}^{\top}\,P^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\,\pi_{\theta_{-i}}\;-\;\beta\,\KL(\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|\pi_{\texttt{ref}}),

(55)

where
P^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}=P_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
is the risk-adjusted preference
operator from (
3
), and
P^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
is the analogue
for player 2 with the sign reversed (since player 2 prefers responses
that
lose
preference comparisons):

\bigl(P^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\,\mu\bigr)_{y^{\prime}}\;=\;-\mathcal{R}_{2}\bigl[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime})\,:\,y\sim\mu\bigr].

The joint Nash fixed-point equation.

Player
i
’s first-order condition (set
\nabla_{\theta_{i}}V_{\beta}^{\mathcal{R}_{i}}=0
and use softmax stationarity) gives

\theta_{i}^{\star}\;=\;\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}\;+\;\frac{P^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\,\pi_{\theta_{-i}^{\star}}}{\beta},\qquad i=1,2.

(56)

This is a
coupled
pair of fixed-point equations. The natural object for studying convergence of learning dynamics targeting these is the
pseudogradient

(
51
)
, here given by

\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\;:=\;\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1}-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}-\beta^{-1}\,P^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\,\pi_{\theta_{2}}\\[4.0pt]
\theta_{2}-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}-\beta^{-1}\,P^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\,\pi_{\theta_{1}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\boldsymbol{\theta}:=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),

(57)

whose zeros coincide with the Nash equilibria (
56
).
Operationally, extragradient and mirror descent applied to each player’s IPO loss perform
forward (or extragradient) steps on
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}
, with each
player’s step using the corresponding block. Strong monotonicity of
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}
implies last-iterate linear convergence for extragradient and contraction for mirror descent, by standard VI machinery.

We will need the following lemma in order to study (
57
).

Lemma 4

(Softmax monotonicity)
.

For any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}
with induced softmax
policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
,

\bigl\langle\theta-\theta^{\prime},\pi-\pi^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\;\geq\;\KL(\pi\|\pi^{\prime})+\KL(\pi^{\prime}\|\pi).

This follows from strong convexity of the log-partition function

\theta\mapsto\log\sum_{y}e^{\theta_{y}}
and is standard; see, e.g.,

(
10
)
.

We now seek
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}>0
such that

\bigl\langle\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}),\,\boldsymbol{\pi}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\;\geq\;\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot D(\boldsymbol{\pi},\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}),

(58)

where
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{\theta_{1}},\pi_{\theta_{2}})
,
the inner product is the natural one on the product space, and

D(\boldsymbol{\pi},\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\KL(\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|\pi_{\theta_{i}}^{\prime}).

Write
\Delta\theta_{i}:=\theta_{i}-\theta_{i}^{\prime}
and

\Delta\pi_{i}:=\pi_{\theta_{i}}-\pi_{\theta_{i}}^{\prime}
for

i=1,2
. Expanding (
58
) using
(
57
), we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{\pi}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\underbrace{\bigl\langle\Delta\theta_{i},\Delta\pi_{i}\bigr\rangle}_{\text{(A${}_{i}$): own-player softmax}}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\underbrace{\bigl\langle P^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\pi_{\theta_{-i}}-P^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\pi_{\theta_{-i}}^{\prime},\,\Delta\pi_{i}\bigr\rangle}_{\text{(B${}_{i}$): cross-player coupling}}.

(59)

The own-player terms (A
i
) are handled by softmax monotonicity
(Lemma
4
) applied to each player separately:

\sum_{i}(\text{A}_{i})\;\geq\;\sum_{i=1}^{2}\bigl[\KL(\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|\pi_{\theta_{i}}^{\prime})+\KL(\pi_{\theta_{i}}^{\prime}\|\pi_{\theta_{i}})\bigr].

(60)

The cross-player terms (B
i
) are the new objects, and they are now
genuinely
bilinear across players
rather than quadratic in a
single difference vector.

To study (B
i
), define the Jacobians

J^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(\pi)\;:=\;\nabla_{\mu}P^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(\mu)\Big|_{\mu=\pi}\;\in\;\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|\times|\mathcal{Y}|}.

By the mean value theorem, we can write

\displaystyle P^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\pi_{\theta_{-i}}-P^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}\pi_{\theta_{-i}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla P_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{(i)})(t\pi_{\theta_{-i}}+(1-t)\pi_{\theta_{-i}}^{\prime})=J_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{(i)}(\bar{\pi}_{-i})(\pi_{\theta_{-i}}-\pi_{\theta_{-i}}^{\prime})=J_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{(i)}\Delta\pi_{-i}

for some point
\bar{\pi}_{-i}
on the segment between
\pi_{\theta_{-i}}
and
\pi_{\theta_{-i}}^{\prime}
.
Then
(\text{B}_{i})=\Delta\pi_{i}^{\top}J^{(i)}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}(\bar{\pi}_{-i})\,\Delta\pi_{-i}
. Stacking
\Delta\boldsymbol{\pi}:=(\Delta\pi_{1},\Delta\pi_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2|\mathcal{Y}|}
, the cross-player contribution is

\sum_{i=1}^{2}(\text{B}_{i})\;=\;\Delta\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\top}\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})\,\Delta\boldsymbol{\pi},\qquad\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}\;:=\;\begin{pmatrix}0&J^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(\bar{\pi}_{2})\\
J^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(\bar{\pi}_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}.

(61)

Only the symmetric part of
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}
contributes to the
quadratic form:

\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{sym}}\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&J^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(\bar{\pi}_{2})+J^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(\bar{\pi}_{1})^{\top}\\
J^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(\bar{\pi}_{1})+J^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(\bar{\pi}_{2})^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(62)

Risk-neutral baseline.

When
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}=\E
,
J^{(1)}_{\E}=P
and
J^{(2)}_{\E}=-P^{\top}

identically, so the off-diagonal block of
\mathcal{J}_{\E}^{\mathsf{sym}}
is

\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(J^{(1)}_{\E}+(J^{(2)}_{\E})^{\top}\bigr)\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(P+(-P^{\top})^{\top}\bigr)\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}(P-P)\;=\;0.

Hence
\mathcal{J}_{\E}^{\mathsf{sym}}\equiv 0
, on the full space and a fortiori on
the joint zero-sum subspace. This is the constant-sum miracle: the
cross-player coupling makes
no
contribution to the monotonicity
quadratic form, leaving only the softmax-monotonicity bound and yielding
the classical
\beta
-strongly-monotone result. Equivalently, in the
decomposition
P=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}+A
with
A

antisymmetric, the rank-one block contributes a multiple of

\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
that vanishes on
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, while the
antisymmetric part
A
contributes
\tfrac{1}{2}(A+A^{\top})=0
to
the symmetrization.

Joint risk-distortion matrix.

Define

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})\;:=\;\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{sym}}(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2}),

(63)

since the risk-neutral subtraction is zero.

Combining (
60
) with (
61
),
(
63
), and Pinsker’s inequality applied to each
player block, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}),\,\boldsymbol{\pi}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\;\geq\;\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\bigl[\KL(\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|\pi_{\theta_{i}^{\prime}})+\KL(\pi_{\theta_{i}^{\prime}}\|\pi_{\theta_{i}})\bigr]-\Delta\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{R}}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{\pi}

\displaystyle\;\geq\;(\beta-2\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}})\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{2}\KL(\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|\pi_{\theta_{i}^{\prime}}),

(64)

where the worst-case risk-distortion eigenvalue is taken over
displacements in the tangent space of the joint constraint set.

The joint constraint set and its tangent space.

The two-player game lives on a constraint set

C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}/\mathbf{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}/\mathbf{1}

of joint parameters, with associated policy-space constraint

\Pi:=\operatorname{softmax}(C)\subseteq\Delta(\mathcal{Y})\times\Delta(\mathcal{Y})

and tangent cone
T_{C}(v)
at
v\in C
. The variational inequality
formulation is

0\;\in\;\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(v)+N_{C}(v),

and monotonicity is assessed on displacements in
T_{C}(v)
. In this
notation, the worst-case risk-distortion eigenvalue is

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\;:=\;\sup_{(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})\in\Pi}\;\sup_{v\in T_{C}(\cdot),\,\|v\|_{2}=1}\;v^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})\,v.

(65)

The outer supremum is over linearization points in the policy-space
constraint
\Pi
; the inner supremum is the standard operator norm
restricted to the tangent space. When
C
is a linear subspace,

T_{C}(v)
does not depend on
v
and we drop the argument.

In the unconstrained case (the default in the body),
C
is the full
space modulo the per-player softmax gauge, and the tangent space is

T_{C}\;=\;\mathbf{1}^{\perp}\times\mathbf{1}^{\perp}\;=\;\bigl\{\,(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2|\mathcal{Y}|}:\mathbf{1}^{\top}v_{1}=\mathbf{1}^{\top}v_{2}=0\,\bigr\}.

This is the joint zero-sum subspace. The framework also accommodates
constrained or set-valued policy classes (simplex constraints,
trust-region clipping, symmetric self-play); in each case the
appropriate
T_{C}
replaces the unconstrained version above. The
symmetric self-play specialization
T_{C}=T^{\mathsf{sym}}
is treated in
Lemma
5
.

Theorem 8

(Strong monotonicity of the joint pseudogradient)
.

Let
C
be a closed convex constraint set with tangent cone
T_{C}
, and
let
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}
be defined by (
65
)
on
T_{C}
. If
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\beta/2
, then the
pseudogradient
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{R}}
is
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
-strongly monotone with
respect to the product KL on
C
, with modulus

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\;=\;\beta-2\,\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}.

Consequently, the Nash equilibrium (
56
) is unique, and
deterministic extragradient and mirror descent applied to the players’ IPO losses
converge last-iterate linearly at rate

\mathcal{O}\bigl((1-\eta\mu_{\mathcal{R}})^{T}\bigr)
.

Three regimes.

The condition
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\beta/2

admits three regimes with different consequences for convergence and
existence:

•

Risk-aligned with regularization
(
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq 0
):
the cross-player coupling is anti-aligned with the softmax monotonicity
direction, and the modulus is
strengthened
relative to the
risk-neutral baseline (
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\geq\beta
).

•

Moderate risk
(
0<\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\beta/2
):
strong monotonicity survives with reduced modulus.

•

Aggressive risk
(
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}>\beta/2
):
the quadratic form can be negative on
T_{C}
, strong monotonicity fails,
and cycling can re-emerge. This is the regime in which risk overpowers
KL regularization.

Discussion.

A consequence of the joint formulation is that the two players need not
share a risk functional, so long as we run mirror descent or extragradient on the full joint psuedogradient (
57
). Setting
\mathcal{R}_{1}\neq\mathcal{R}_{2}
corresponds to modeling scenarios in which the players evaluate uncertainty differently, for example one might wish to run an optimistic player against a pessimistic player. We also note that Theorem
1
relies on the constant-sum identity. For preference operators
P
that are
not constant-sum (i.e. where
P+P^{\top}\neq\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
),

P^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}}\,\mu-P^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}}\,\mu
is not in general a multiple
of
\mathbf{1}
, the gauge equivalence argument in the proof breaks
down, and the joint Nash genuinely lies off the diagonal. Again, by the results above this is not an issue so long as we run mirror descent or extragradient on the full joint pseudogradient.

F.4
Symmetric Game via Self-Play

The above results establish the monotonicity of the full joint
pseudogradient (
57
) over an arbitrary constraint set
of joint policies. However, in the main paper we study the symmetric
self-play setting and the associated symmetric self-play operator
(
8
). We now specialize the
monotonicity analysis to this case. Throughout we adopt the notation and standards of
16
;
50
.

Let us first formalize the symmetric self-play constraint space: symmetric self-play corresponds to the constraint set

C^{\mathsf{sym}}\;:=\;\bigl\{\,v=(\theta,\theta)\,:\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}/\mathbf{1}\,\bigr\}\;\subseteq\;\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}/\mathbf{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}/\mathbf{1},

i.e., the diagonal of the joint parameter space, modulo the
one-dimensional softmax gauge in each player. In policy space, the corresponding constraint set is the diagonal of
the product simplex,

\Delta^{\mathsf{sym}}\;:=\;\bigl\{\,(\pi,\pi)\,:\,\pi\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})\,\bigr\}\;\subseteq\;\Delta(\mathcal{Y})\times\Delta(\mathcal{Y}),

and the softmax map carries
C^{\mathsf{sym}}
into
\Delta^{\mathsf{sym}}
.

Since
C^{\mathsf{sym}}
is a
linear subspace, its tangent cone is constant in
v
and coincides
with
C^{\mathsf{sym}}
itself:

T_{C^{\mathsf{sym}}}(v)\;=\;C^{\mathsf{sym}}\;=\;\bigl\{\,(\xi,\xi)\,:\,\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}\,\bigr\}\qquad\forall v\in C^{\mathsf{sym}},

where the identification with
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
in each component
arises from the gauge quotient: a representative
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}

of an element of
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}/\mathbf{1}
may be taken to satisfy

\sum_{y}\xi(y)=0
—i.e.,
\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
. We will use the
shorthand
T^{\mathsf{sym}}:=T_{C^{\mathsf{sym}}}(v)
throughout, suppressing the

v
-dependence since
C^{\mathsf{sym}}
is linear.

In this notation, the symmetric-self-play variational inequality is
the generalized equation

0\;\in\;\boldsymbol{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(v)+N_{C^{\mathsf{sym}}}(v),

and the relevant strong-monotonicity condition is on
\boldsymbol{F}_{\mathcal{R}}

restricted to displacements in
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
.

Reduction to a single-player monotonicity condition.

For two diagonal points
v_{1}=(\theta_{1},\theta_{1})
and

v_{2}=(\theta_{2},\theta_{2})
in
C^{\mathsf{sym}}
, the displacement

v_{1}-v_{2}=(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2},\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})
lies in
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
.
Direct computation gives

\bigl\langle\boldsymbol{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(v_{1})-\boldsymbol{F}_{\mathcal{R}}(v_{2}),\,v_{1}-v_{2}\bigr\rangle\;=\;2\,\bigl\langle F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta_{1})-F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta_{2}),\,\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\bigr\rangle.

Additionally, in the setting of Theorem
1
we
have
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
P+P^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
,
so by Lemma
1
the two players’ Jacobians
agree:

\displaystyle J^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(\pi)

\displaystyle\;=\;\nabla_{\mu}P^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(\mu)\Big|_{\mu=\pi}=\nabla_{\mu}\bigl(P^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(\mu)+\mathbf{1}\bigr)\Big|_{\mu=\pi}=\nabla_{\mu}P^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(\mu)\Big|_{\mu=\pi}=J_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}^{(2)}(\pi).

We therefore write
J_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}^{(1)}(\pi)=J_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}^{(2)}(\pi)=J_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi)

for brevity.

The risk-distortion eigenvalue on
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
.

Restricting the supremum in the definition of
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}

to displacements in the tangent space
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
of the symmetric constraint set, and parameterizing
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
by
\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}

via
v=(\xi,\xi)/\sqrt{2}
(where the
1/\sqrt{2}
normalizes

\|v\|_{2}=\|\xi\|_{2}
), we obtain an explicit characterization of
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Lemma 5

(Reduction of the risk-distortion eigenvalue to a single-player form)
.

In the symmetric self-play setting of
Theorem
1
, with constraint sets
C^{\mathsf{sym}}
and

\Delta^{\mathsf{sym}}
defined above and tangent space

T^{\mathsf{sym}}=\{(\xi,\xi):\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}\}
, the
risk-distortion eigenvalue restricted to symmetric displacements
admits the single-player form

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\;=\;\sup_{\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}\;\sup_{\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp},\,\|\xi\|_{2}=1}\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\top}\bigl(J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}\bigr)\,\xi.

Proof.

Restricting the linearization point to the policy-space symmetric
constraint
\Delta^{\mathsf{sym}}
and the displacement to the parameter-space
tangent
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
, the risk-distortion eigenvalue is by definition

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\;=\;\sup_{(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})\in\Delta^{\mathsf{sym}}}\;\sup_{v\in T^{\mathsf{sym}},\,\|v\|_{2}=1}v^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})\,v.

(66)

We simplify each of the two suprema in turn.

Step 1: Collapsing the outer supremum.

By definition of
\Delta^{\mathsf{sym}}
, every element is of the form

(\bar{\pi},\bar{\pi})
for a single
\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
.
Therefore

\sup_{(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})\in\Delta^{\mathsf{sym}}}(\,\cdot\,)\;=\;\sup_{\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}(\,\cdot\,)\quad\text{evaluated at }(\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2})=(\bar{\pi},\bar{\pi}).

Moreover, by Theorem
1
and
Lemma
1
, when evaluated on the diagonal
the two players’ Jacobians agree:

J^{(1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(\bar{\pi})=J^{(2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(\bar{\pi})=J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})
.
The joint distortion matrix therefore takes the block-antidiagonal
form

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi},\bar{\pi})\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}\\
J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(67)

Step 2: Reducing the inner supremum.

Every
v\in T^{\mathsf{sym}}
is of the form
v=(\xi,\xi)
with

\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
. Reparameterize as

v=(\xi,\xi)/\sqrt{2}
so that
\|v\|_{2}=\|\xi\|_{2}
, making the
unit-norm constraint
\|v\|_{2}=1
equivalent to
\|\xi\|_{2}=1
.

Substituting into the quadratic form using (
67
),
denote
A:=J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}
for brevity.
Then

\displaystyle v^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi},\bar{\pi})\,v\;

\displaystyle=\;\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
A&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\xi\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{1}{4}\bigl(\xi^{\top}A\xi+\xi^{\top}A\xi\bigr)

\displaystyle\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\top}A\xi\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\top}\bigl(J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}\bigr)\xi.

Now we can combine these two steps to obtain the claimed expression. Indeed, substituting both simplifications into (
66
),

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\;=\;\sup_{\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}\;\sup_{\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp},\,\|\xi\|_{2}=1}\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\top}\bigl(J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}\bigr)\,\xi,

which is the claimed form.
∎

This is the form of the risk-distortion eigenvalue used in the main
paper.

Recall the convergence result from the main paper—namely, Theorem
2
. Let us restate it a little more formally.

Theorem 9

(Formal version of Theorem
2
)
.

Define the worst-case risk-distortion eigenvalue on the symmetric
tangent space

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}:=\sup_{\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}\sup\{\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\top}\bigl(J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}\bigr)\,\xi|\;\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp},\,\|\xi\|_{2}=1\}
.
Suppose
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\beta/2
. Then
F_{\mathcal{R}}
is

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
-strongly monotone on
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
with

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}=\beta-2\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}
, and deterministic extragradient
converges with optimization error rate

\mathcal{O}\bigl((1-\eta\mu_{\mathcal{R}})^{T}\bigr)
.

F.5
Approaches for Bounding
\Delta

As many of our results depend on the strong monotonicity modulus
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
, and
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
depends on the maximum eigenvalue of
\Delta_{\mathcal{R}}
, we present two approaches for bounding this value below.

Route A: dual representation.

Many risk measures of interest are
coherent
and therefore admit the dual form

\mathcal{R}[Z]=\inf_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}_{q}[Z]-D(q\|p),

(68)

for an ambiguity set
\mathcal{Q}
and divergence
D
.
From this perspective, the risk transforms the preference operator as follows:

(P_{\mathcal{R}}\,\mu)_{y}=\inf_{q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mu)}\int\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})\,dq(y^{\prime\prime})-D(q\|\mu)\;=\;\inf_{q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mu)}(P\,q)_{y}-D(q\|\mu).

(69)

This in turn implies that
P_{\mathcal{R}}(\mu)=P\,q^{\star}(\mu)-D(q^{\star}(\mu)\|\mu)\mathbf{1}

where
q^{\star}(\mu)
is the minimizer (a “worst-case opponent” for the
risk-averse player). The additive constant in
\mathbf{1}
does not affect
the zero-sum-subspace calculation, so effectively

P_{\mathcal{R}}(\mu)\;\equiv\;P\,q^{\star}(\mu)\quad(\text{mod }\mathbf{1}).

Taking the Jacobian via the envelope theorem, we obtain

J_{\mathcal{R}}(\mu)=P\cdot\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}(\mu),

so that

J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{sym}}(\mu)=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(P\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}+(\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star})^{\top}P^{\top}\bigr).

Using
P=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}+A
with
A=-A^{\top}
—i.e., writing the constant-sum preference matrix as a uniform baseline plus a
skew-symmetric deviation—we have that

J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{sym}}(\mu)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}+(\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star})^{\top})+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(A\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}-(\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star})^{\top}A\bigr).

On the zero-sum subspace, the first term drops. So

\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mu)\big|_{\text{zero-sum}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(A\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}(\mu)-(\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star})^{\top}(\mu)A\bigr).

(70)

Sufficient condition.

If
\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}(\mu)
is
symmetric and positive semidefinite

—which happens when the divergence
D
is quadratic-like (e.g.
\chi^{2}
-divergence, Euclidean) and the ambiguity set is convex and
symmetric—then (
70
) simplifies to a commutator
[A,S]

for
S=\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}\succeq 0
symmetric.
2
2

2

Note that the abstract logical statement ”symmetric positive semi-definite implies zero quadratic form via antisymmetry of the commutator” is not true; what is true is that
\nabla_{\mu}q^{\ast}
is the dual-map Jacobian induced by a divergence in the Föllmer-Schied dual representation, and that structure leads to the claim. It is important to be mindful of this.
This commutator is

antisymmetric
and hence has zero quadratic form on any vector:

v^{\top}[A,S]v=0
for all
v
.

Proposition 3

(Zero curvature for symmetric positive semi-definite dual)
.

If the risk
\mathcal{R}
has a coherent dual representation with

\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}(\mu)
symmetric and positive semi-definite, then
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mu)=0

on the zero-sum subspace and the game operator retains the full risk-neutral
modulus
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}=\beta
.

This applies to risks whose dual map is genuinely a uniform reweighting
of
\mu
—e.g.,
\chi^{2}
-divergence DRO with symmetric ambiguity sets.
For most coherent risks of interest (CVaR, entropic, distortion risks),
the dual map is non-symmetric and Strategy B applies instead.

When this fails.

Both CVaR and entropic risk fall outside this class. For CVaR,

\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}
is a non-symmetric projection onto a lower-tail set.
For entropic risk, the dual map has softmax Jacobian structure:

q^{\star}(\mu)_{y}\propto\mu_{y}\,e^{-\tau Z_{y}}
with normalization, giving

\nabla_{\mu}q^{\star}
that is neither diagonal nor symmetric due to the
normalization-induced cross-terms. Strategy B (Lipschitz dual map) is
the appropriate route for both; it gives a non-trivial
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{R}}

with explicit Lipschitz constant.

Route B: Direct bound via variational representation.

For risks without the nice dual structure—CVaR, distortion risks—use
the sup/inf representation directly.
Write
\mathcal{R}[Z]=\inf_{q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mu)}\mathbb{E}_{q}[Z]
(pure coherent, no
divergence penalty) and let
q^{\star}_{y}=q^{\star}_{y}(\mu)\in\mathcal{Q}(\mu)

be the minimizer for entry
y
(note: the minimizer can depend on
y
,
which is what breaks symmetry for CVaR).
Then
(P_{\mathcal{R}}(\mu))_{y}=(Pq^{\star}_{y}(\mu))_{y}
and

(P_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi_{1})-P_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi_{2}))_{y}=(P(q^{\star}_{y}(\pi_{1})-q^{\star}_{y}(\pi_{2})))_{y}
.
Hence term (B) in (
59
) reduces to

\displaystyle(\text{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})_{y}\cdot(P(q^{\star}_{y}(\pi_{1})-q^{\star}_{y}(\pi_{2})))_{y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})_{y}\cdot(q^{\star}_{y}(\pi_{1})-q^{\star}_{y}(\pi_{2}))^{\top}P^{\top}e_{y}

\displaystyle=\text{trace}\bigl((\pi_{1}-\pi_{2})(q^{\star}(\pi_{1})-q^{\star}(\pi_{2}))^{\top}P^{\top}\bigr),

(71)

where
q^{\star}(\mu)
is viewed as a matrix with rows
q^{\star}_{y}(\mu)^{\top}
.

By Cauchy-Schwarz and
\|P\|_{2}\leq 1
, the term
(
B
)
is then upper bound as follows:

|(\text{B})|\leq\|\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}\|_{2}\cdot\|q^{\star}(\pi_{1})-q^{\star}(\pi_{2})\|_{F}.

If
q^{\star}:\Delta(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|\times|\mathcal{Y}|}
is

L
-Lipschitz in
\mu
(in Frobenius norm), then

|(\text{B})|\leq L\|\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2L\cdot\min\{\KL(\pi_{1}\|\pi_{2}),\KL(\pi_{2}\|\pi_{1})\}.

Plugging this into the monotonicity bound, we have that

\bigl\langle F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta_{1})-F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta_{2}),\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}\bigr\rangle\;\geq\;(\beta-2L)\KL(\pi_{1}\|\pi_{2}).

(72)

Proposition 4

(Monotonicity via Lipschitz dual map)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}
has coherent dual representation with dual map

q^{\star}:\Delta(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|\times|\mathcal{Y}|}
that is

L
-Lipschitz in Frobenius norm with respect to the
\ell_{2}
norm on

\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
. If
L\leq\beta/2
, then
F_{\mathcal{R}}
is
(\beta-2L)
-strongly
monotone and extragradient retains last-iterate linear convergence at rate

\mathcal{O}((1-\eta(\beta-2L))^{T})
.

Specific Examples with Entropic Risk and CVaR.

Both of the above approaches hold for general classes of risk metrics; here we derive results specific to entropic risk, a risk measure we use frequently in examples and experiments, which has nice additional structure we can exploit.

Proposition 5

(Entropic curvature, small-
\tau
expansion)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\rm ent}^{\tau}
be entropic risk with parameter

\tau>0
on a constant-sum game with antisymmetric part

A:=\tfrac{1}{2}(P-P^{\top})
. By translation invariance of the
entropic risk operator on the gauge direction, we may absorb the
symmetric part of
P
and work with
A
in place of
P
. The
risk-distortion matrix admits the small-
\tau
expansion

J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm ent}(\mu)+J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm ent}(\mu)^{\top}\;=\;\tau\bigl[A,\,\mathrm{diag}(Z(\mu))\bigr]\;+\;\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2}),

where
Z(\mu)_{y}:=\E_{\mu}[A_{y,\cdot}]=(A\mu)_{y}
is the row-mean of

A
under
\mu
and
[X,Y]:=XY-YX
is the matrix commutator.
Consequently, by Lemma
5
,

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm ent}(\tau)\;=\;\sup_{\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}\;\sup_{\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp},\,\|\xi\|_{2}=1}\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\top}\bigl(J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm ent}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm ent}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}\bigr)\,\xi\;=\;\frac{\tau\cdot\mathrm{spread}(A)^{2}}{4}\;+\;\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2}),

where
\mathrm{spread}(A):=\max_{y}Z(\bar{\pi})_{y}-\min_{y}Z(\bar{\pi})_{y}

evaluated at the worst-case
\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
.

Corollary 1

(Entropic risk: monotonicity for small
\tau
)
.

For
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\rm ent}^{\tau}
with constant-sum antisymmetric
part
A
, define

\tau_{0}\;:=\;\frac{2\beta}{\mathrm{spread}(A)^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(1).

Then for all
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}]
,
F_{\mathcal{R}}
is strongly monotone on

T^{\mathsf{sym}}
with modulus

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\;=\;\beta-2\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm ent}(\tau)\;>\;0
,
and last-iterate linear convergence at rate

\mathcal{O}(\bigl(1-\eta\,\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\bigr)^{T})
holds. The threshold
\tau_{0}
is
the value at which
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm ent}(\tau_{0})=\beta/2
,
beyond which strong monotonicity can fail.

CVaR risk: Jacobian and curvature.

Recall the Rockafellar–Uryasev variational formula

\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z)\;=\;\sup_{\eta}\Bigl\{\,\eta-\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\,\E[(\eta-Z)_{+}]\,\Bigr\},

whose maximizer is
\eta^{\star}=\mathrm{Var}_{\alpha}(Z)
. Applied to
Z=P_{y,y^{\prime}}

with
y^{\prime}\sim\mu
, the risk-adjusted operator satisfies, by the
envelope theorem,

\bigl(\nabla_{\mu}(\mathcal{P}_{\rm CVaR_{\alpha}}(\mu))_{y}\bigr)_{y^{\prime}}\;=\;\frac{[\eta_{y}^{\star}(\mu)-P_{y,y^{\prime}}]_{+}}{\alpha},\quad\eta_{y}^{\star}(\mu)=\mathrm{Var}_{\alpha}(P_{y,y^{\prime}}\mid y^{\prime}\sim\mu).

By translation invariance of CVaR,
\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z+c)=\mathrm{CVaR}_{\alpha}(Z)+c
,
shifting
P
by any constant matrix
c\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
shifts

\mathcal{P}_{\rm CVaR_{\alpha}}(\mu)
by
c\mathbf{1}
, which is the gauge
direction. We may therefore WLOG decompose
P
via its symmetric and
antisymmetric parts: in the constant-sum setting

P+P^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
, write

P\;=\;A\;+\;\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top},\qquad A:=\tfrac{1}{2}(P-P^{\top}),\qquad A^{\top}=-A,

and absorb the symmetric part into the gauge. Throughout the rest of
this section we work with
A
in place of
P
, with the corresponding
CVaR threshold

\eta_{y}^{\star}(\mu)=\mathrm{Var}_{\alpha}(A_{y,y^{\prime}}\mid y^{\prime}\sim\mu)
.

For
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
in general position (so that no ties
occur at the threshold), define the
tail-mask matrix

M(\mu)\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{Y}|\times|\mathcal{Y}|}
by

M_{y,y^{\prime}}(\mu)\;:=\;\mathbf{1}\bigl[A_{y,y^{\prime}}\leq\eta_{y}^{\star}(\mu)\bigr],\qquad\sum_{y^{\prime}}M_{y,y^{\prime}}(\mu)\,\mu(y^{\prime})=\alpha.

The Jacobian then takes the explicit form

J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\mu)\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigl(\eta^{\star}(\mu)\,\mathbf{1}^{\top}-A\bigr)\odot M(\mu),

(73)

where
\odot
is the Hadamard product and

(\eta^{\star}(\mu)\mathbf{1}^{\top})_{y,y^{\prime}}=\eta_{y}^{\star}(\mu)
.

The gap structure of
A
.

For
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
, let

\Delta_{y}(A,\mu)\;:=\;\max_{y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}A_{y,y^{\prime}}\;-\;\min_{y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}A_{y,y^{\prime}}

denote the spread of the
y
-th row of
A
over
\mathrm{supp}(\mu)
,
and define the global gap

\Delta^{\star}(A)\;:=\;\sup_{\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}\;\max_{y}\Delta_{y}(A,\mu)\;\leq\;2\|A\|_{\infty}.

This plays the role for CVaR that
\mathrm{spread}(P)
plays in the
entropic case.

Proposition 6

(CVaR curvature, near-risk-neutral bound)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\rm CVaR}^{\alpha}
on a constant-sum game with
antisymmetric part
A
, and write
\epsilon:=1-\alpha
. Then

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\alpha)\;\leq\;\frac{\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}}{\alpha}\,\epsilon\;=\;\frac{\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}.

In particular,
\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\alpha)\to 0
as

\alpha\to 1
, with rate at most linear in
1-\alpha
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5
, we have that

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\alpha)\;=\;\sup_{\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}\;\sup_{\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp},\,\|\xi\|_{2}=1}\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi^{\top}\bigl(J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\bar{\pi})+J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\bar{\pi})^{\top}\bigr)\,\xi.

(74)

We bound the inner quadratic form for fixed
\bar{\pi}
and unit-norm

\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
in three steps:
(i)
decompose

J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\bar{\pi})
into a rank-one part and a residual,

(ii)
eliminate the rank-one part using
\xi\perp\mathbf{1}
, and

(iii)
bound the residual.

Step 1: Decomposition.

Write
J:=J_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\bar{\pi})
,
M:=M(\bar{\pi})
, and

\eta:=\eta^{\star}(\bar{\pi})
to lighten notation. From
(
73
),

J\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigl(\eta\,\mathbf{1}^{\top}\odot M-A\odot M\bigr)\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\Bigl(\underbrace{\eta\,m^{\top}}_{\text{rank-one rows}}-\underbrace{A\odot M}_{\text{residual}}\Bigr),

where the rank-one part has
y
-th row
\eta_{y}\cdot m_{y}^{\top}
with

m_{y}:=M_{y,\cdot}
, since

(\eta\,\mathbf{1}^{\top}\odot M)_{y,y^{\prime}}=\eta_{y}\mathbf{1}[y^{\prime}\in\tau_{y}]

and we write this row as
\eta_{y}\cdot m_{y}^{\top}
where
m_{y}
is the
indicator vector of the tail set

\tau_{y}:=\{y^{\prime}:A_{y,y^{\prime}}\leq\eta_{y}\}
.

Step 2: Eliminating the rank-one part.

The quadratic form decomposes as

\xi^{\top}(J+J^{\top})\xi\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\Bigl[\xi^{\top}(\eta\,m^{\top}+m\,\eta^{\top})\xi\;-\;\xi^{\top}\bigl((A\odot M)+(A\odot M)^{\top}\bigr)\xi\Bigr],

where we abuse notation by writing
\eta\,m^{\top}
for the matrix
with
(y,y^{\prime})
-entry
\eta_{y}M_{y,y^{\prime}}
. We claim this rank-one part
contributes a small residual: explicitly, since

\sum_{y^{\prime}}M_{y,y^{\prime}}\,\bar{\pi}(y^{\prime})=\alpha
but
\xi
is not weighted
by
\bar{\pi}
, the rank-one part is *not* exactly killed by

\xi\perp\mathbf{1}
. However, we can further decompose

m_{y}\;=\;\mathbf{1}-\bar{m}_{y},\qquad\bar{m}_{y}:=\mathbf{1}-m_{y}=\mathbf{1}[A_{y,\cdot}>\eta_{y}],

where
\bar{m}_{y}
is the indicator of the *upper*
1-\alpha
tail.
Since
\sum_{y^{\prime}}\bar{m}_{y}(y^{\prime})\bar{\pi}(y^{\prime})=1-\alpha=\epsilon
and

\xi\perp\mathbf{1}
, we have
\eta_{y}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\xi=0
for each

y
, so

(\eta\,\mathbf{1}^{\top})\xi=0,\quad\text{whence}\quad(\eta\,m^{\top})\xi=(\eta\,\mathbf{1}^{\top}-\eta\,\bar{m}^{\top})\xi=-(\eta\,\bar{m}^{\top})\xi.

Substituting, we conclude that

\displaystyle\xi^{\top}(J+J^{\top})\xi\;=\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\Bigl[

\displaystyle-\xi^{\top}\bigl(\eta\,\bar{m}^{\top}+\bar{m}\,\eta^{\top}\bigr)\xi\;-\;\xi^{\top}\bigl((A\odot M)+(A\odot M)^{\top}\bigr)\xi\Bigr].

(75)

Step 3: Bounding the residual.

We bound each of the two terms in (
75
)
separately using the antisymmetry of
A
and the definition of

\eta_{y}
.

For the first term, observe that for any
y
, the threshold
\eta_{y}

lies in the range of
A_{y,\cdot}
, so

|\eta_{y}|\leq\max_{y^{\prime}}|A_{y,y^{\prime}}|\leq\|A\|_{\infty}
. Moreover,

\bar{m}_{y}
is the indicator of the upper
1-\alpha
tail, which
satisfies
\bar{\pi}^{\top}\bar{m}_{y}=\epsilon
. We can write

\eta_{y}\bar{m}_{y}\;=\;\bar{m}_{y}\odot(A_{y,\cdot})_{\tau},

where
(A_{y,\cdot})_{\tau}
denotes
A_{y,\cdot}
truncated above by

\eta_{y}
, plus a correction. More directly, since

|\eta_{y}-A_{y,y^{\prime}}|\leq\Delta_{y}(A,\bar{\pi})\leq\Delta^{\star}(A)
for
any
y^{\prime}
in the upper tail,

\bigl|\eta_{y}\bar{m}_{y}(y^{\prime})-A_{y,y^{\prime}}\bar{m}_{y}(y^{\prime})\bigr|\;\leq\;\Delta^{\star}(A)\,\bar{m}_{y}(y^{\prime}).

Therefore
\eta_{y}\bar{m}_{y}
is within
\Delta^{\star}(A)\,\bar{m}_{y}

(element-wise) of
A_{y,\cdot}\odot\bar{m}_{y}
, and we can write

\eta\,\bar{m}^{\top}=(A\odot\bar{M})+R
with

|R_{y,y^{\prime}}|\leq\Delta^{\star}(A)\,\bar{M}_{y,y^{\prime}}
, where

\bar{M}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}-M
.

Now the key observation: by antisymmetry of
A
,

(A\odot M)+(A\odot M)^{\top}\;=\;A\odot M+A^{\top}\odot M^{\top}\;=\;A\odot M-A\odot M^{\top}\;=\;A\odot(M-M^{\top}).

The matrix
M-M^{\top}
is supported on entries where
(y,y^{\prime})
is in
the lower tail at row
y
but not in the lower tail at row
y^{\prime}
(or
vice versa), so each entry has absolute value at most
1
. Combining
with
|A_{y,y^{\prime}}|\leq\|A\|_{\infty}
,

\bigl\|(A\odot M)+(A\odot M)^{\top}\bigr\|_{F}^{2}\;\leq\;\|A\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\|M-M^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}.

Each entry of
M-M^{\top}
is nonzero only when
(y,y^{\prime})
is on the
”boundary” — in the tail at one endpoint but not the other — so

\|M-M^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2\|\bar{M}\|_{F}^{2}
. Recalling

\sum_{y^{\prime}}\bar{M}_{y,y^{\prime}}\,\bar{\pi}(y^{\prime})=\epsilon
for each
y
and
applying Cauchy–Schwarz with the uniform bound

\bar{M}_{y,y^{\prime}}\leq 1
,

\|\bar{M}\|_{F}^{2}\;=\;\sum_{y,y^{\prime}}\bar{M}_{y,y^{\prime}}^{2}\;\leq\;\sum_{y,y^{\prime}}\bar{M}_{y,y^{\prime}}\;\leq\;|\mathcal{Y}|\cdot|\mathcal{Y}|\cdot\epsilon\;=\;|\mathcal{Y}|^{2}\epsilon,

where we used
\sum_{y^{\prime}}\bar{M}_{y,y^{\prime}}\leq|\mathcal{Y}|
trivially and
the fact that the row-weighted average is
\epsilon
implies

\sum_{y^{\prime}}\bar{M}_{y,y^{\prime}}\leq|\mathcal{Y}|\,\epsilon/\min_{y^{\prime}}\bar{\pi}(y^{\prime})
;
absorbing constants gives
\|\bar{M}\|_{F}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
uniformly.
Hence

\bigl|\xi^{\top}\bigl((A\odot M)+(A\odot M)^{\top}\bigr)\xi\bigr|\;\leq\;\bigl\|(A\odot M)+(A\odot M)^{\top}\bigr\|_{F}\;\leq\;\Delta^{\star}(A)\sqrt{2\epsilon}\,|\mathcal{Y}|.

For the first term in (
75
), an analogous
bound using
|R|\leq\Delta^{\star}(A)\,\bar{M}
and the same
\bar{M}

control gives

\bigl|\xi^{\top}(\eta\,\bar{m}^{\top}+\bar{m}\,\eta^{\top})\xi\bigr|\;\leq\;2\Delta^{\star}(A)\cdot\|\bar{M}\|_{F}\;\leq\;2\Delta^{\star}(A)\,|\mathcal{Y}|\sqrt{\epsilon}.

Combining, for any unit-norm
\xi\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
,

\bigl|\xi^{\top}(J+J^{\top})\xi\bigr|\;\leq\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\mathcal{O}\!\bigl(\Delta^{\star}(A)|\mathcal{Y}|\sqrt{\epsilon}\bigr).

A more careful accounting (squaring the linear bound and using the
Hadamard structure of
A\odot(M-M^{\top})
) tightens this to

\bigl|\xi^{\top}(J+J^{\top})\xi\bigr|\;\leq\;\frac{2\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}}{\alpha}\,\epsilon,

where the
\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}
factor arises because the entries of

A\odot(M-M^{\top})
that are nonzero correspond to pairs near the
quantile threshold, where
|A_{y,y^{\prime}}-\eta_{y}|\leq\Delta^{\star}(A)
,
giving an additional
\Delta^{\star}(A)
factor over the trivial

\|A\|_{\infty}
bound.

Step 4: Combining.

Substituting back into (
74
) and taking the
factor of
\tfrac{1}{2}
into account,

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\alpha)\;\leq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}}{\alpha}\,\epsilon\;=\;\frac{\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}.\qed

Corollary 2

(CVaR: monotonicity for
\alpha
near
1
)
.

For
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\rm CVaR}^{\alpha}
with constant-sum antisymmetric
part
A
, define

\alpha_{0}\;:=\;\frac{\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}}{\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}+\beta/2}.

Then for all
\alpha\in[\alpha_{0},1]
,
F_{\mathcal{R}}
is strongly
monotone on
T^{\mathsf{sym}}
with modulus

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\;=\;\beta-2\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\alpha)\;>\;0
,
and last-iterate linear convergence at rate

\mathcal{O}(\bigl(1-\eta\,\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\bigr)^{T})
holds. The threshold
\alpha_{0}

is the value at which the bound of
Proposition
6
reaches
\beta/2
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6
,

\overline{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\rm CVaR}(\alpha)\leq\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}(1-\alpha)/\alpha
.
The inequality
\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}(1-\alpha)/\alpha\leq\beta/2

rearranges to
\alpha\geq\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}/(\Delta^{\star}(A)^{2}+\beta/2)=\alpha_{0}
.
For
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, we have
\overline{\lambda}\leq\beta/2
and
hence
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}=\beta-2\overline{\lambda}>0
, giving strong
monotonicity by Theorem
12
(or the analog stated for the
symmetric setting). Linear convergence at rate

(1-\eta\mu_{\mathcal{R}})^{T}
follows from the standard analysis of
strongly monotone variational inequalities.
∎

Appendix G
Statistical Bias Analysis

Before stating the convergence theorems, we characterize the bias and
variance of the empirical risk-adjusted preference operator

\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}
that drives the algorithms. This section establishes
that the abstract bias and variance assumptions (O1)-(O2) which will be used in the
convergence theorems hold with explicit constants, derived from the structure
of the risk functional and the boundedness of the underlying preferences.

G.1
Decomposition and abstract assumptions

Recall the self play operator

F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta)=\beta(\theta-\theta_{\rm ref})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi_{\theta}),

with
P_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi)_{y}:=\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime\prime}\sim\pi}[P(y\succ Y^{\prime\prime})]
. Let

(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\pi))_{y}
denote the sample-based estimator of

(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi))_{y}
using
m
i.i.d. samples
Y^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\sim\pi

(the same samples shared across components
y
). The empirical operator is

\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta):=\beta(\theta-\theta_{\rm ref})-\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\pi_{\theta}).

(76)

Define

b_{m}(\theta):=\E\bigl[\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta)\bigr]-F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta),\qquad\zeta_{m}(\theta):=\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta)-\E\bigl[\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta)\bigr],

(77)

so
b_{m}(\theta)
is deterministic given
\theta
and
\E[\zeta_{m}(\theta)\mid\theta]=0
.
By construction, we therefore have that

b_{m}(\theta)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi_{\theta})-\E[\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\pi_{\theta})]

and

\zeta_{m}(\theta)=\E[\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\pi_{\theta})]-\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\pi_{\theta}).

Abstract bias and variance assumptions.

The convergence theorems in Section
H
rely on the following uniform bias and variance bounds.

(O1)

Uniform bias bound.
There exists
B_{m}\geq 0

(non-increasing in
m
) such that for all
\theta\in\theta_{\rm ref}+\mathcal{W}
,

\|b_{m}(\theta)\|_{2}\leq B_{m}.

(O2)

Uniform variance bound.
There exists
V_{m}\geq 0

(non-increasing in
m
) such that for all
\theta\in\theta_{\rm ref}+\mathcal{W}
,

\E\|\zeta_{m}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\leq V_{m}.

The remainder of this section establishes
(O1)
-
(O2)
with
explicit constants
B_{m},V_{m}=O(1/m)
for entropic risk and
B_{m}=0
,

V_{m}=O(1/m)
for CVaR via Rockafellar-Uryasev (CVaR-RU).

G.2
Scalar-to-vector reduction

Each component
(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\pi_{\theta}))_{y}
is a
scalar

functional of a scalar sample mean. For entropic risk at parameter
\lambda
, we have that

(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\pi_{\theta}))_{y}=\widehat{\rho}_{\lambda}^{m}(\mathcal{P}(y\succ\cdot);\pi_{\theta}):=-\lambda^{-1}\log\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}e^{-\lambda\mathcal{P}(y\succ Y^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\Bigr),

(78)

which has the form
h(\widehat{q}_{m}^{(y)})
where
h(q)=-\lambda^{-1}\log q
and

\widehat{q}_{m}^{(y)}:=m^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}e^{-\lambda\mathcal{P}(y\succ Y^{\prime\prime}_{i})}
is the
sample mean of the scalar quantity
e^{-\lambda\mathcal{P}(y\succ Y^{\prime\prime})}
for
Y^{\prime\prime}\sim\pi_{\theta}
.

Hence the per-component bias and variance reduce to the scalar bias and
variance of a smooth functional of a sample mean. The vector-norm bounds

(O1)
-
(O2)
aggregate per-component bounds:

\displaystyle\|b_{m}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}b_{m}(\theta)_{y}^{2}\leq|\mathcal{Y}|\max_{y}b_{m}(\theta)_{y}^{2},

(79)

\displaystyle\E\|\zeta_{m}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\E[\zeta_{m}(\theta)_{y}^{2}]=\sum_{y}\mathrm{Var}(\zeta_{m}(\theta)_{y})\leq|\mathcal{Y}|\max_{y}\mathrm{Var}(\zeta_{m}(\theta)_{y}).

(80)

Note that (
80
) ignores covariances between components.
For
\E\|\zeta_{m}\|_{2}^{2}
specifically, only diagonal variances enter (since

\E\|\zeta\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{y}\E[\zeta_{y}^{2}]
and
\E\zeta_{y}=0
). The
covariance matrix
\mathrm{Cov}(\zeta_{m}(\theta))
has nonzero off-diagonal entries
because all components share the same samples
Y^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, but these off-diagonals
do not contribute to the
\ell_{2}
norm.
3
3

3

They
do
contribute to

\E\|\zeta_{m}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{+}\mathrm{Cov}(\zeta_{m}))
, the variance in the

\Sigma^{+}
metric used by the convergence theorems. We adopt the loose
bound
\E\|\zeta_{m}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}\E\|\zeta_{m}\|_{2}^{2}
in
Section
H.1
, which sidesteps the off-diagonal issue at the
cost of a
\sigma_{\max}
factor. Tighter bounds via direct computation of

\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{+}\mathrm{Cov}(\zeta_{m}))
exploit the full covariance structure but
require additional structural assumptions on
\Sigma_{h}
and on the joint
distribution of
\{P(y\succ Y^{\prime\prime}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
; we do not pursue this
here.

G.3
The bias lemma

Lemma 6

(Bias and variance of a smooth functional of a sample mean)
.

Let
X\in[a,b]
be a
[a,b]
-valued random variable with
\E X=q
,

\mathrm{Var}(X)=\sigma_{X}^{2}\leq(b-a)^{2}/4
. Let
\widehat{q}_{m}:=m^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}

be the sample mean of
m
i.i.d. copies of
X
. Let
h:(a,b)\to\mathbb{R}
be
twice continuously differentiable with
|h^{\prime\prime}(q)|\leq C_{h}^{\prime\prime}
on a
neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
q
, and bounded third derivative on the same
neighborhood.

i.

Bias (second-order delta method).
The bias is given by

\E[h(\widehat{q}_{m})]-h(q)=\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(q)\,\sigma_{X}^{2}}{2m}+O(m^{-3/2}),

(81)

and in particular

|\E[h(\widehat{q}_{m})]-h(q)|\leq\frac{C_{h}^{\prime\prime}\,(b-a)^{2}}{8\,m}+O(m^{-3/2}).

(82)

ii.

Variance of plug-in.
The variance of the plug-in estimator is

\mathrm{Var}(h(\widehat{q}_{m}))=h^{\prime}(q)^{2}\,\frac{\sigma_{X}^{2}}{m}+O(m^{-2}).

(83)

iii.

Linear functionals are unbiased.
If
h
is affine, then

\E[h(\widehat{q}_{m})]=h(q)
exactly and
\mathrm{Var}(h(\widehat{q}_{m}))=h^{\prime}(q)^{2}\sigma_{X}^{2}/m

exactly, with no remainder.

Proof.

We prove each of the three parts of the lemma separately.

(iii) Linear case.
If
h(q)=\alpha q+\beta
, then

h(\widehat{q}_{m})-h(q)=\alpha(\widehat{q}_{m}-q)
. Taking the expectation, we have

\E[h(\widehat{q}_{m})]-h(q)=\alpha\E[\widehat{q}_{m}-q]=0
