, and the bias estimator satisfies

(B1)
-
(B3)
. Run
TT-EG

(Algorithm
2
) with constant step sizes
\eta,\gamma

satisfying

\eta\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}\,L_{G}},\qquad\gamma\in(0,1],

(230)

where
L_{G}=\sigma_{\max}\ell_{\mathcal{R}}
. Assume the debiased gradient norm
is bounded:
\|\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,i)}\|_{2}\leq G
uniformly along the
trajectory. Then for all
T\geq 1
, the averaged iterate

\widehat{z}_{T}:=T^{-1}\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}w_{\tau}
satisfies

\E[\mathrm{Gap}_{\mathrm{vi}}(\widehat{z}_{T})]\;\leq\;\frac{\Omega^{2}}{T\eta}\;+\;\frac{7\eta}{2}\bigl(\sigma_{\max}\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m}\bigr)\;+\;2\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,\mathcal{E}_{T},

(231)

where
\mathcal{E}_{T}:=\sup_{\tau\leq T}\sqrt{\E\|e^{*}_{\tau-1}\|_{2}^{2}}

is the worst-case root-mean-square tracking error, satisfying (by
Lemma
18
, after burn-in
T\gg 1/\gamma
)

\mathcal{E}_{T}\;\leq\;\sqrt{V_{\infty}}\;\leq\;\frac{\sqrt{2C_{1}}\,\eta}{\gamma}\;+\;\sqrt{2C_{2}}\,R_{m}\;+\;\sqrt{2C_{3}\gamma V_{m}^{b}}.

(232)

Comparison to the un-tracked case.

Theorem
11
(un-tracked) had bound

\Omega^{2}/(T\eta)+(7\eta/2)(\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})+2\Omega\widetilde{B}_{m}
,
with a persistent
\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{B}_{m})=\mathcal{O}(B_{m})
bias floor that does
not decay in
T
. Theorem
16
replaces both
occurrences of
\widetilde{B}_{m}
by
\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,\mathcal{E}_{T}
,
where
\mathcal{E}_{T}
scales as
\eta/\gamma+R_{m}+\sqrt{\gamma}\sqrt{V_{m}^{b}}

rather than as
B_{m}
. This is the same kind of bias-floor reduction
as in Theorem
13
: with
\eta=\mathcal{O}(\gamma/m)
and

\gamma
constant,
\mathcal{E}_{T}=\mathcal{O}(1/m+R_{m}+1/m^{3/2})=\mathcal{O}(1/m)

for delta-method estimators (driven by the
1/m
timescale-gap term);
pushing further to
\eta=\mathcal{O}(\gamma/m^{2})
drives the timescale gap
below the
\mathcal{O}(1/m^{3/2})
noise filter, giving

\mathcal{E}_{T}=\mathcal{O}(1/m^{3/2})
— the same factor-
\sqrt{m}
improvement
over the un-tracked
\mathcal{O}(1/m)
floor that we derived in the
strongly-monotone constant-step setting (cf. the
“Tuned step sizes” paragraph following Theorem
14
).

Comparison to the strongly-monotone case.

The structural difference between Theorem
16
and
Theorem
13
is where the tracking error

\sqrt{V_{\tau}}
enters the bound:

•

Strongly-monotone, time-varying step
(Thm.
13
):

\sqrt{V_{\tau}}
enters via a
contraction-weighted average
\sum_{\tau}w_{\tau}^{T}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}
, which
inherits the trajectory’s
\limsup
rather than its
\sup
(via
Lemma
17
). Under decaying
\eta_{t}/\gamma_{t}\to 0
,
the bound is asymptotically
\mathcal{O}(R_{m})
—i.e., vanishing in
T
to the
irreducible bias-of-bias-estimator residual.

•

Strongly-monotone, constant step (Thm.
14
):

\sqrt{V_{\tau}}
enters via a geometric weighting that is closed-form
summable, giving the four-term steady-state floor of
(
215
) (timescale gap, residual bias,
noise filter, slow variance), persistent in
T
.

•

Monotone, constant step (Thm.
16
):

\sqrt{V_{\tau}}
enters as a uniform
\sup_{\tau}
, giving the persistent
floor
2\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,\mathcal{E}_{T}
in
(
231
), persistent in
T
.

The monotone-only analysis can only give an averaged-iterate
\mathcal{O}(1/T)

gap rate, not last-iterate linear contraction; the tracking error
appears as a uniform sup rather than inside a geometric weighting that
would allow a vanishing-in-
T
bound. The four-term floor structure
of Theorem
14
survives qualitatively (trade-off
between
\eta/\gamma
,
R_{m}
,
\sqrt{\gamma}
,
\eta
), but the
asymptotic vanishing under decaying step sizes does not.

Proof of Theorem
16
.

The proof parallels Theorem
11
, with the noise/bias
quantities recomputed for the debiased gradient

\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,i)}=\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau,i)}-\xi_{\tau-1}
.

Step 1: deterministic-style bound.

Apply Lemma
12
with the debiased gradients

\zeta=\eta\Sigma\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,1)}
,
\eta=\eta\Sigma\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,2)}
.
The proof of Theorem
10
carries through verbatim
with
\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau,i)}
replaced by
\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,i)}
,
giving the same
\Gamma(t)
structure as in (
134
):

\mathrm{Gap}_{\mathrm{vi}}(\widehat{z}_{T})\leq\frac{1}{T\eta}\Gamma(T),\quad\Gamma(T)=2\Theta(z_{c})+\sum_{\tau}\frac{3\eta^{2}}{2}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r_{\tau-1}}^{2}+2\eta^{2}\sum_{\tau}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{\tau}}^{2}+\sum_{\tau}\langle\eta\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau},y_{\tau-1}-w_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma^{+}},

(233)

where now
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau}:=G(w_{\tau})-\Sigma\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,2)}

and
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{z}:=\|\widetilde{g}_{\rm eval}-G(z)\|_{\Sigma^{+}}

are the noise/bias of the
debiased
oracle.

Step 2: conditional moments of the debiased oracle.

Decompose
\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,2)}=\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau,2)}-\xi_{\tau-1}

with
\xi_{\tau-1}=b_{m}(w_{\tau})+e^{*}_{\tau-1}
:

\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,2)}=\bigl(\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau,2)}-b_{m}(w_{\tau})-F_{\mathcal{R}}(w_{\tau})\bigr)+F_{\mathcal{R}}(w_{\tau})-e^{*}_{\tau-1}.

The conditional mean (on
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
) of
\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau,2)}-b_{m}(w_{\tau})-F_{\mathcal{R}}(w_{\tau})

is zero by definition of
b_{m}
. Hence

\E[\widetilde{g}^{(\tau,2)}-F_{\mathcal{R}}(w_{\tau})\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]=-e^{*}_{\tau-1},

i.e., the conditional bias of the debiased oracle is
-e^{*}_{\tau-1}
, with

\Sigma^{+}
-norm
\leq\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\|e^{*}_{\tau-1}\|_{2}
. The
conditional variance is
\mathrm{Var}(\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau,2)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau})\leq V_{m}

(unchanged — subtracting the
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
-measurable
\xi_{\tau-1}
does
not affect variance). Translating to
\Sigma^{+}
-norm gives

\mathrm{Var}(\widetilde{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau})\leq\sigma_{\max}V_{m}=\widetilde{V}_{m}
.

Step 3: bound
\E\widetilde{\varepsilon}^{2}
.

By bias-variance decomposition in
\Sigma^{+}
norm, we have that

\E\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{\tau}}^{2}=\E\|\E[\widetilde{g}-G(w_{\tau})\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}+\E\|\widetilde{g}-\E[\widetilde{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}\E\|e^{*}_{\tau-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m}.

By the analogous decomposition at
r_{\tau-1}
(with
e^{*}_{\tau-1}

replaced by
\xi_{\tau-1}-b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})
, whose mean square is also

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2})
up to drift):

\E\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r_{\tau-1}}^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m}

(absorbing the
\mathcal{O}(\eta)
drift into
\mathcal{E}_{T}
). Summing the noise
contributions and dividing by
T\eta
, we deduce that

\frac{1}{T\eta}\sum_{\tau}\Bigl[\tfrac{3\eta^{2}}{2}\E\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r_{\tau-1}}^{2}+2\eta^{2}\E\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{\tau}}^{2}\Bigr]\leq\tfrac{7\eta}{2}\bigl(\sigma_{\max}\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m}\bigr).

Step 4: bound the cross term
\E\langle\eta\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau},y_{\tau-1}-w_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma^{+}}
.

Following Bound 2 of Theorem
11
, by the tower property

\E\langle\eta\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau},y_{\tau-1}-w_{\tau}\rangle=\eta\E\langle\E[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau}],y_{\tau-1}-w_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma^{+}}
.
By Step 2,
\|\E[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]\|_{\Sigma^{+}}=\|\Sigma e^{*}_{\tau-1}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}\leq\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\|e^{*}_{\tau-1}\|_{2}
.
Cauchy-Schwarz with the
\Sigma^{+}
-diameter
\|y_{\tau-1}-w_{\tau}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}\leq 2\Omega
:

|\E\langle\eta\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau},y_{\tau-1}-w_{\tau}\rangle_{\Sigma^{+}}|\leq 2\eta\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,\E\|e^{*}_{\tau-1}\|_{2}\leq 2\eta\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,\mathcal{E}_{T},

using Jensen. Summing and dividing by
T\eta
contributes
2\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,\mathcal{E}_{T}
.

Step 5: combine.

\E\Gamma(T)/(T\eta)
is bounded by
2\Theta(z_{c})/(T\eta)=\Omega^{2}/(T\eta)

plus the noise contribution from Step 3 plus the cross-term contribution
from Step 4, giving (
231
). The bound
(
232
) on
\mathcal{E}_{T}
follows from
Lemma
18
(which is a specialization of
Theorem
14
(i) to the monotone regime, valid because
that part of the proof does not use strong monotonicity).
∎

Tuned rate.

Substituting the tuned
\mathcal{E}_{T}=\mathcal{O}(1/m^{3/2})
from the
“Tuned step sizes” paragraph after Theorem
14
(with

\eta=\mathcal{O}(\gamma/m^{2})
and
T\gtrsim m
burn-in):

\E[\mathrm{Gap}_{\mathrm{vi}}(\widehat{z}_{T})]=O\bigl(\Omega^{2}/(T\eta)\bigr)+O\bigl(\eta\widetilde{V}_{m}\bigr)+O\bigl(\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}/m^{3/2}\bigr),

(234)

where the first two terms give the standard
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{Tm}+1/T)

rate under the variance/Lipschitz balance
\eta\sim 1/\sqrt{Tm}
, and
the third is the reduced bias floor. This is the merely-monotone
analog of (
218
); the rate to the bias floor is the
same in both regimes, but the floor itself is persistent in
T
here
(no asymptotic-vanishing analog of Theorem
13

is available without strong monotonicity).

Implications by risk type.

•

Entropic risk:

R_{m}=\mathcal{O}(e^{2\lambda}/(\lambda m^{2}))
,

V_{m}^{b}=\mathcal{O}(e^{4\lambda}/(\lambda^{2}m^{3}))
. Two timescale extraradient drives the persistent

\mathcal{O}(e^{2\lambda}/(\lambda m))
floor of the un-tracked monotone case
down to
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{E}_{T})=\mathcal{O}(e^{2\lambda}/m^{3/2})
in the appropriate
step-size regime—a full power of
1/m
improvement, matching the
strongly-monotone constant-step case. Unlike the strongly-monotone
time-varying-step Theorem
13
, no further
improvement to
\mathcal{O}(R_{m})=\mathcal{O}(1/m^{2})
is available here, because the
weighted-average argument that drives the floor to
R_{m}
requires
strong monotonicity.

•

CVaR via Rockafellar-Uryasev:

R_{m}=V_{m}^{b}=0

identically. The bias estimator
\xi^{*}\equiv 0
and

\mathcal{E}_{T}\to\mathcal{O}(\eta/\gamma)
, which can be made arbitrarily
small. Two timescale extragradient reduces to un-tracked extragradient at
\xi\equiv 0
, recovering
Theorem
11
exactly with
\widetilde{B}_{m}=0
.

I.4
Two-Timescale Mirror Descent: Corollary from extra-gradient analysis

We now state the analogue of Theorems
13

and
14
for stochastic projected Mirror Descent with
two-timescale bias tracking, obtained by specializing the extragradient analysis
to the case where the extrapolation step is omitted. As in
Section
H.10
, the structural cost of dropping
extrapolation is an additional Lipschitz-times-step-size floor that
extragradient’s two-call structure cancels exactly; otherwise the proofs reuse
the technical lemmas of Section
I
(scalar
two-timescale unrolling, weighted-average analysis) directly.

Algorithm.

The two-timescale Mirror Descent algorithm
proceeds as follows: set
r_{0}\in D
and
\xi_{0}=0
, then for

\tau=1,\ldots,T
:

\displaystyle\text{Sample at }r_{\tau-1}:\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau)},\widehat{b}_{m}^{(\tau)},

\displaystyle r_{\tau}\leftarrow\Pi_{D}\bigl(r_{\tau-1}-\eta_{\tau}\,\Sigma\,(\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau)}-\xi_{\tau-1})\bigr),

(235)

\displaystyle\xi_{\tau}\leftarrow(1-\gamma_{\tau})\xi_{\tau-1}+\gamma_{\tau}\,\widehat{b}_{m}^{(\tau)}.

The output is
r_{T}
in the strongly monotone case, or

\widehat{z}_{T}=T^{-1}\sum_{\tau}r_{\tau-1}
in the monotone case.
Each iteration uses a single oracle call (vs. two for extragradient), with

\mathcal{F}_{\tau}:=\sigma(r_{0},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{\tau})
the natural
filtration.

Tracking error.

Define

e_{\tau}:=\xi_{\tau}-b_{m}(r_{\tau})
and the mean-square tracking error

V_{t}:=\E\|e_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}
. The single-step structure of mirror descent simplifies
the drift analysis: with only one oracle call per iteration, there is
no extrapolation point
w_{\tau}
to track, and
\delta_{\tau}
reduces to
the simpler form
\delta_{\tau}:=(1-\gamma_{\tau})b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+\gamma_{\tau}b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})-b_{m}(r_{\tau})=b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})-b_{m}(r_{\tau})
.
5
5

5

This
is structurally simpler than the extragradient drift

\delta_{\tau}^{\rm extragradient}=(1-\gamma_{\tau})b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+\gamma_{\tau}b_{m}(w_{\tau})-b_{m}(r_{\tau})
,
which had to interpolate between the extrapolation and the leader’s
new state.

I.4.1
Strongly monotone case, decaying steps

Corollary 9

(Two-timescale mirror descent, strongly monotone, decaying steps)
.

Suppose
F_{\mathcal{R}}
is
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
-strongly monotone and

\ell_{\mathcal{R}}
-Lipschitz, the gradient oracle satisfies

(O1)
–
(O2)
, and the bias estimator satisfies

(B1)
–
(B3)
. Let

\{\eta_{t},\gamma_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
be deterministic, predictable sequences
satisfying:

\eta_{t}\leq\min\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}},\,\frac{1}{2L_{G}}\Bigr\},\quad\gamma_{t}\in(0,1],\quad\frac{\eta_{t}}{\gamma_{t}}\to 0,\quad\gamma_{t}\to 0,\quad\sum_{t\geq 1}\eta_{t}=\infty.

(236)

Assume the debiased gradient norm is bounded along the trajectory:

\|\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau)}-\xi_{\tau-1}\|_{2}\leq G
uniformly.

i.

Fast-timescale tracking.
The error
V_{t}
satisfies the
non-asymptotic recurrence

V_{t+1}\leq(1-\gamma_{t}/2)\,V_{t}+\frac{C_{1}^{\rm md}\,\eta_{t}^{2}}{\gamma_{t}}+C_{2}\,\gamma_{t}\,R_{m}^{2}+C_{3}\,\gamma_{t}^{2}\,V_{m}^{b},

(237)

where
C_{1}^{\rm md}=7(C_{\rm drift}^{\rm md})^{2}
,
C_{2}=6
,
C_{3}=2
,
and
C_{\rm drift}^{\rm md}=L_{b}\sigma_{\max}G
(smaller than the
extragradient constant
C_{\rm drift}=3L_{b}\sigma_{\max}G
since mirror descent has only
one oracle call). Consequently

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{t}}\leq\sqrt{2C_{2}}\,R_{m}=2\sqrt{3}\,R_{m}.

(238)

ii.

Slow-timescale recurrence.
The slow iterates obey the
per-step recurrence

\E V(r_{\tau},\theta^{\star})\leq(1-\eta_{\tau}\widetilde{\mu})\,\E V(r_{\tau-1},\theta^{\star})+2\eta_{\tau}\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}+\eta_{\tau}^{2}(\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})+4\eta_{\tau}^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}.

(239)

iii.

Asymptotic vanishing bias.
Combining
(i)
and

(ii)
via the weighted-average argument of
Lemmas
15
–
17
,

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\E\|r_{T}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq\frac{4\sqrt{3}\,\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}}{\widetilde{\mu}}\cdot R_{m}.

(240)

Proof.

We work step by step, paralleling the proof of
Theorem
13
with simplifications from the
single-call mirror descent structure.

Step 1: tracking-error recurrence.

The bias tracker’s update is

\xi_{\tau}=(1-\gamma_{\tau})\xi_{\tau-1}+\gamma_{\tau}\widehat{b}_{m}^{(\tau)}
,
with
\widehat{b}_{m}^{(\tau)}=b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+r_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+\nu^{(\tau)}

(by the same decomposition as in Step 1 of the proof of
Theorem
13
). Defining
e_{\tau}:=\xi_{\tau}-b_{m}(r_{\tau})
, we have

\displaystyle e_{\tau}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma_{\tau})(e_{\tau-1}+b_{m}(r_{\tau-1}))+\gamma_{\tau}b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+\gamma_{\tau}r_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+\gamma_{\tau}\nu^{(\tau)}-b_{m}(r_{\tau})

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma_{\tau})e_{\tau-1}+\delta_{\tau}^{\rm md}+\gamma_{\tau}r_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+\gamma_{\tau}\nu^{(\tau)},

where the mirror descent drift is

\delta_{\tau}^{\rm md}:=b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})-b_{m}(r_{\tau}).

By Lipschitz continuity of
b_{m}
(
(B3)
) and the mirror descent step bound

\|r_{\tau}-r_{\tau-1}\|_{2}\leq\eta_{\tau}\sigma_{\max}G
(from
non-expansiveness of
\Pi_{D}
and the bounded gradient assumption) we have that

\|\delta_{\tau}^{\rm md}\|_{2}\leq L_{b}\|r_{\tau-1}-r_{\tau}\|_{2}\leq L_{b}\sigma_{\max}G\,\eta_{\tau}=:C_{\rm drift}^{\rm md}\,\eta_{\tau}.

Step 2: mean-square recurrence.

The mean-square computation follows Step 2 of the proof of
Theorem
13
verbatim with
C_{\rm drift}
replaced by

C_{\rm drift}^{\rm md}
and the cross-term analysis simplified (since

\delta_{\tau}^{\rm md}
does not depend on the correction-step samples,
the inner-product is mean-zero by independence; we keep the same
upper bound for uniformity). Repeating verbatim the steps of the
extragradient proof yields (
237
) with

C_{1}^{\rm md}=7(C_{\rm drift}^{\rm md})^{2}=7L_{b}^{2}\sigma_{\max}^{2}G^{2}
,

C_{2}=6
,
C_{3}=2
.

Step 3: asymptotic envelope on
V_{t}
.

Apply Lemma
14
(scalar two-timescale unrolling) with

u_{t}=V_{t}
,
c=1/2
, and
F_{t}=C_{1}^{\rm md}\eta_{t}^{2}/\gamma_{t}+C_{2}\gamma_{t}R_{m}^{2}+C_{3}\gamma_{t}^{2}V_{m}^{b}
to get that

\frac{F_{t}}{\gamma_{t}}=\frac{C_{1}^{\rm md}\eta_{t}^{2}}{\gamma_{t}^{2}}+C_{2}R_{m}^{2}+C_{3}\gamma_{t}V_{m}^{b}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}C_{2}R_{m}^{2},

using
\eta_{t}/\gamma_{t}\to 0
and
\gamma_{t}\to 0
. The lemma gives

\limsup_{t}V_{t}\leq 2C_{2}R_{m}^{2}=12R_{m}^{2}
, hence
(
238
). This proves part
(i)
.

Step 4: slow-timescale per-step recurrence.

Apply the prox-mapping descent inequality (
112
) of
Lemma
11
with
z=r_{\tau-1}
,
\xi=\eta_{\tau}\widetilde{G}_{m}^{(\tau)}

(where
\widetilde{G}_{m}^{(\tau)}:=\Sigma\widetilde{g}^{(\tau)}=\Sigma(\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau)}-\xi_{\tau-1})
),

w=r_{\tau}
, and
u=\theta^{\star}
to get that

V(r_{\tau},\theta^{\star})\leq V(r_{\tau-1},\theta^{\star})+\eta_{\tau}\langle\widetilde{G}_{m}^{(\tau)},\theta^{\star}-r_{\tau-1}\rangle_{\Sigma^{+}}+\tfrac{\eta_{\tau}^{2}}{2}\|\widetilde{G}_{m}^{(\tau)}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}.

(241)

Inner product term.
Following Step 2 of the proof of
Theorem
7
and Step 2 of the proof of
Theorem
13
jointly: decompose

\widetilde{g}^{(\tau)}=\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau)}-\xi_{\tau-1}
where

\xi_{\tau-1}=b_{m}(r_{\tau-1})+e_{\tau-1}
(note the index shift:
the tracker at the start of iteration
\tau
is the iterate of the
fast-timescale dynamic at time
\tau-1
). Using the

\Sigma\Sigma^{+}=\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}
identity of
Lemma
8
(i), we deduce that

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{G}_{m}^{(\tau)},\theta^{\star}-r_{\tau-1}\rangle_{\Sigma^{+}}

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau)}-\xi_{\tau-1},\theta^{\star}-r_{\tau-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle F_{\mathcal{R}}(r_{\tau-1}),\theta^{\star}-r_{\tau-1}\rangle+\text{(noise + bias terms)}

\displaystyle\quad-\langle e_{\tau-1},\theta^{\star}-r_{\tau-1}\rangle.

By
(P-strong)
and the equilibrium condition

F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta^{\star})\in\ker\Sigma
(Step 3 of the proof of
Theorem
7
), we have that

\langle F_{\mathcal{R}}(r_{\tau-1}),\theta^{\star}-r_{\tau-1}\rangle\leq-2\widetilde{\mu}V(r_{\tau-1},\theta^{\star}).

Squared gradient term.
By Step 3 of the proof of
Theorem
7
, we have

\|\widetilde{G}_{m}^{(\tau)}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq 8L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}+2\|\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2},

where
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau}:=G(r_{\tau-1})-\widetilde{G}_{m}^{(\tau)}
,
and
\E\|\widetilde{\Delta}_{\tau}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m}+\sigma_{\max}\E\|e_{\tau-1}\|_{2}^{2}

by bias-variance decomposition incorporating the tracking error.

Bias cross-term.
Following Step 6 (Term 2) of the proof of
Theorem
7
and Step 4 of the proof of
Theorem
13
: the new tracking-error contribution

\eta_{\tau}\langle e_{\tau-1},r_{\tau-1}-\theta^{\star}\rangle
is bounded
in expectation by
2\eta_{\tau}\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}

via Cauchy–Schwarz, the metric translation

\|v\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\|v\|_{\Sigma^{+}}
, the

\Sigma^{+}
-diameter
\|r_{\tau-1}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}\leq 2\Omega
,
and Jensen’s inequality

\E\|e_{\tau-1}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\E\|e_{\tau-1}\|_{2}^{2}}=\sqrt{V_{\tau}}
.

Next we combine these terms by taking conditional then unconditional expectation, dropping
non-positive terms via
\eta_{\tau}\leq 1/(2L_{G})
to absorb the

8\eta_{\tau}^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}
piece into
4\eta_{\tau}^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}
as
in the proof of Theorem
7
) to get that

\E V(r_{\tau},\theta^{\star})\leq(1-\eta_{\tau}\widetilde{\mu})\E V(r_{\tau-1},\theta^{\star})+2\eta_{\tau}\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}+\eta_{\tau}^{2}(\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})+4\eta_{\tau}^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2},

(242)

which is (
239
). Note that this matches the extragradient
recurrence (
200
) except for the additional

4\eta_{\tau}^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}
Lipschitz floor; this is the structural
penalty for omitting extrapolation, identical to the gap noted in
Section
H.10
.

Step 5: telescoping and weighted-average analysis.

Iterating (
242
) yields

\displaystyle\E V(r_{T},\theta^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{t=1}^{T}(1-\eta_{t}\widetilde{\mu})\,V(r_{0},\theta^{\star})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}\Bigl[\prod_{s=\tau+1}^{T}(1-\eta_{s}\widetilde{\mu})\Bigr]\Bigl(2\eta_{\tau}\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}+\eta_{\tau}^{2}(\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})+4\eta_{\tau}^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\Bigr).

(243)

The bias contribution is analyzed exactly as in Step 6 of the proof of
Theorem
13
: defining the weights

w_{\tau}^{T}:=\widetilde{\mu}\eta_{\tau}\prod_{s=\tau+1}^{T}(1-\eta_{s}\widetilde{\mu})

of (
208
), the bias term becomes

(2\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}/\widetilde{\mu})\sum_{\tau}w_{\tau}^{T}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}
.
Lemmas
15
–
17
apply
verbatim (their statements depend only on the kernel structure

(1-\eta_{s}\widetilde{\mu})
, not on whether mirror descent or extragradient produces it), giving

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}w_{\tau}^{T}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}\leq\limsup_{\tau}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}\leq 2\sqrt{3}\,R_{m}

by (
238
). Hence we have that

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}\Bigl[\prod_{s>\tau}(1-\eta_{s}\widetilde{\mu})\Bigr]2\eta_{\tau}\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\sqrt{V_{\tau}}\leq\frac{4\sqrt{3}\,\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,R_{m}}{\widetilde{\mu}}.

(244)

Step 6: variance, Lipschitz continuity, and initial-condition terms.

The variance contribution

(\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})\sum_{\tau}[\prod_{s>\tau}(1-\eta_{s}\widetilde{\mu})]\eta_{\tau}^{2}

is bounded by Step 7 of the proof of Theorem
13
:

\eta_{\tau}^{2}\leq\eta_{\tau}\overline{\eta}_{T}
, the telescoping identity
gives
\sum_{\tau}[\prod_{s>\tau}(1-\eta_{s}\widetilde{\mu})]\eta_{\tau}\leq 1/\widetilde{\mu}
,
and
\overline{\eta}_{T}\to 0
since
\eta_{t}\to 0
. Hence the variance
contribution vanishes in
T
.

The Lipschitz contribution

4L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\sum_{\tau}[\prod_{s>\tau}(1-\eta_{s}\widetilde{\mu})]\eta_{\tau}^{2}\leq 4L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\overline{\eta}_{T}/\widetilde{\mu}\to 0

by the same argument. This is the new term relative to extragradient: it vanishes
asymptotically under the schedule (
236
), but at
the same rate as the variance contribution (i.e.,
\overline{\eta}_{T}
).

The initial-condition term satisfies

\prod_{t}(1-\eta_{t}\widetilde{\mu})\leq\exp(-\widetilde{\mu}\sum_{t}\eta_{t})\to 0

since
\sum_{t}\eta_{t}=\infty
.

Step 7: combine.

Multiplying (
243
) by
2
to convert from

V(\cdot,\cdot)
to
\|\cdot\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}
and taking

\limsup_{T\to\infty}
yields

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\E\|r_{T}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq 0+\frac{4\sqrt{3}\,\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}}{\widetilde{\mu}}\cdot R_{m}+0+0,

where the four contributions are: initial-condition (vanishing),
bias-tracking (residual
R_{m}
), variance (vanishing), Lipschitz
(vanishing). This is (
240
), completing
part
(iii)
.
∎

Comparison to extragradient (Theorem
13
).

The
asymptotic bound (
240
) matches extragradient’s bound
(
202
)
exactly
: both have leading constant

4\sqrt{3}\,\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}/\widetilde{\mu}
and floor
R_{m}
.
This is because the new mirror descent Lipschitz term
4\eta_{\tau}^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}

vanishes in the asymptotic limit at the same rate as the variance term,
and both are absorbed into the “vanishing” part of the bound. The mirror descent
proof has slightly tighter constants in the tracking recurrence
(
C_{1}^{\rm md}=L_{b}^{2}\sigma_{\max}^{2}G^{2}\cdot 7
vs.
C_{1}=9L_{b}^{2}\sigma_{\max}^{2}G^{2}\cdot 7
for extragradient) since the
single-call structure produces a smaller drift. The price for these
smaller constants and the simpler algorithm is the additional
Lipschitz term in the slow-timescale recurrence, but this cost is
invisible asymptotically.

I.4.2
Strongly monotone case, constant steps

Corollary 10

(Two-timescale mirror descent, strongly monotone, constant steps)
.

Under the assumptions of Corollary
9
except
with constant step sizes
\eta_{\tau}\equiv\eta
,

\gamma_{\tau}\equiv\gamma
satisfying

\eta\leq\min\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2\widetilde{\mu}},\,\frac{1}{2L_{G}}\Bigr\},\qquad\gamma\in(0,1],

(245)

the iterates of (
235
) satisfy:

(i)

For all
T\geq 1
,

\E\|r_{T}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq(1-\eta\widetilde{\mu})^{T}\|r_{0}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\\
+\frac{4\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}}{\widetilde{\mu}}\Bigl(\sqrt{V_{0}}(1-\eta\widetilde{\mu})^{T}+\frac{K_{1}^{\rm md}\eta}{\gamma}+K_{2}R_{m}+K_{3}\sqrt{\gamma V_{m}^{b}}\Bigr)\\
+\frac{2\eta(\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})}{\widetilde{\mu}}+\frac{8L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\eta}{\widetilde{\mu}},

(246)

with
K_{1}^{\rm md}=\sqrt{2C_{1}^{\rm md}}=\sqrt{14}\,L_{b}\sigma_{\max}G
,

K_{2}=\sqrt{2C_{2}}=2\sqrt{3}
,
K_{3}=\sqrt{2C_{3}}=2
.

(ii)

As
T\to\infty
,

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\E\|r_{T}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}\leq\\
\underbrace{\frac{4\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}K_{1}^{\rm md}}{\widetilde{\mu}}\cdot\frac{\eta}{\gamma}}_{\text{timescale gap}}+\underbrace{\frac{4\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}K_{2}R_{m}}{\widetilde{\mu}}}_{\text{residual bias}}+\underbrace{\frac{4\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}K_{3}\sqrt{\gamma V_{m}^{b}}}{\widetilde{\mu}}}_{\text{noise filter}}+\underbrace{\frac{2\eta(\widetilde{B}_{m}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})}{\widetilde{\mu}}}_{\text{slow variance}}+\underbrace{\frac{8L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\eta}{\widetilde{\mu}}}_{\text{Lipschitz floor}}.

(247)

Proof.

The proof follows that of Theorem
14
verbatim with two
modifications: the per-step recurrence is (
239
)
in place of (
200
), and the smaller drift constant

C_{\rm drift}^{\rm md}
replaces
C_{\rm drift}
. Telescoping the
geometric recurrence with constant contraction
1-\gamma/2
gives the
finite-horizon tracking bound

V_{t}\leq(1-\gamma/2)^{t}V_{0}+2C_{1}^{\rm md}\eta^{2}/\gamma^{2}+2C_{2}R_{m}^{2}+2C_{3}\gamma V_{m}^{b}

as in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
14
, hence the
square-root bound

\sqrt{V_{t}}\leq\sqrt{V_{0}}(1-\gamma/2)^{t/2}+K_{1}^{\rm md}\eta/\gamma+K_{2}R_{m}+K_{3}\sqrt{\gamma V_{m}^{b}}
.

Telescoping the slow recurrence (
242
) with constant

\eta
yields (
246
) via the geometric-series
argument of Step 2 of the proof of Theorem
14
. The new

4\eta^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}
Lipschitz term contributes

\sum_{\tau}(1-\eta\widetilde{\mu})^{T-\tau}\cdot 4\eta^{2}L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\leq 4\eta L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}/\widetilde{\mu}

via the telescoping identity
\sum_{\tau}(1-\eta\widetilde{\mu})^{T-\tau}\eta\leq 1/\widetilde{\mu}
,
giving the
8L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\eta/\widetilde{\mu}
floor in
(
247
) after the factor of
2
for converting

V
to
\|\cdot\|_{\Sigma^{+}}^{2}
. Taking
T\to\infty
kills the
contraction terms, leaving the five-term floor.
∎

Comparison to constant-step extragradient (Theorem
14
).

The mirror descent bound (
247
) has
five
non-vanishing
floor contributions instead of four: the same timescale-gap, residual-bias,
noise-filter, and slow-variance terms, plus an additional

Lipschitz floor

8L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\eta/\widetilde{\mu}
. This term
is reducible by taking
\eta
small, but it does not vanish for fixed

\eta
. In the asymptotic regime (Corollary
9
)
the schedule
\eta_{t}\to 0
kills this term automatically; in the
constant-step regime, it is the structural cost of dropping
extrapolation. For typical step sizes
\eta=\mathcal{O}(1/L_{G})
this
contributes
\mathcal{O}(L_{G}\Omega^{2})
, of comparable scale to the slow-variance
term
\mathcal{O}(\eta\widetilde{V}_{m})
.

Tuned step sizes.

With
\eta=\mathcal{O}(\gamma/m)
and
\gamma

constant, the timescale-gap and noise-filter analysis from Section
“Tuned step sizes” after Theorem
14
carries through
identically:
\mathcal{E}_{T}=\mathcal{O}(1/m^{3/2})
once
\eta/\gamma\lesssim R_{m}

(i.e.,
T\gtrsim m
iterations), giving the same factor-
\sqrt{m}

improvement over the un-tracked mirror descent floor as in the extragradient case. The
additional Lipschitz term
8L_{G}^{2}\Omega^{2}\eta/\widetilde{\mu}=\mathcal{O}(\eta)

contributes at the same scale as the slow-variance floor and does not
change the leading-order
1/m^{3/2}
rate.

I.4.3
Monotone case, constant steps

Corollary 11

(Two-timescale mirror descent, monotone case, biased oracle with tracking)
.

Suppose
F_{\mathcal{R}}
is monotone (not necessarily strongly monotone) and

\ell_{\mathcal{R}}
-Lipschitz continuous, the oracle satisfies
(O1)
–
(O2)
,
and the bias estimator satisfies
(B1)
–
(B3)
.
Run the two-timescale mirror descent algorithm (
235
) with constant
step sizes
\eta,\gamma
satisfying
\eta\leq 1/L_{G}
,
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
Assume
\|\widehat{F}_{m}^{(\tau)}-\xi_{\tau-1}\|_{2}\leq G
uniformly. Then
the averaged iterate
\widehat{z}_{T}=T^{-1}\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}r_{\tau-1}

satisfies

\E[\mathrm{Gap}_{\mathrm{vi}}(\widehat{z}_{T})]\leq\frac{\Omega^{2}}{T\eta}+\eta(\sigma_{\max}\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2}+\widetilde{V}_{m})+2\Omega\sqrt{\sigma_{\max}}\,\mathcal{E}_{T}+4L_{G}\eta\Omega^{2},

(248)

where
\mathcal{E}_{T}:=\sup_{\tau\leq T}\sqrt{\E\|e_{\tau-1}\|_{2}^{2}}
is
bounded by the right-hand side of (
232
) with

C_{1}^{\rm md}
replacing
C_{1}
.

Proof.

The proof parallels Theorem
16
verbatim, replacing
the extragradient deterministic-style bound with the mirror descent analogue from
Corollary
5
: the structure

\Omega^{2}/(T\eta)+\eta(\cdots)+4L_{G}\eta\Omega^{2}
replaces the extragradient
form
\Omega^{2}/(T\eta)+(7\eta/2)(\cdots)
, and the tracking-error
analysis from Lemma
18
carries through with

C_{\rm drift}^{\rm md}
in place of
C_{\rm drift}
. The
cross-term and squared-bias bounds at the debiased oracle proceed
identically to Steps 3–4 of the proof of Theorem
16
.
∎

Appendix J
Stability and Generalization

We now establish that the risk-adjusted QRE depends Lipschitz-continuously
on the underlying preference operator. Although our softmax
parameterization makes the problem technically unconstrained in

\theta
, the natural framework for this kind of parametric stability is
the variational inequality formulation, with
Lipschitz dependence following from the Dontchev–Rockafellar strong
metric regularity machinery for strongly monotone problems

(
16
)
. This framework is more robust than the
classical implicit function theorem: it does not require differentiability
of the operator at the solution, does not require any non-degeneracy or
interiority condition on active constraints, and extends seamlessly to
constrained or set-valued policy classes.

We present this proof only for the symmetric self play setting, as it can easily be extended to a general (monotone) risk-adjusted game.

Generalized equation formulation.

Identify a policy
parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}
with the policy

\pi_{\theta}=\mathrm{softmax}(\theta)
. The problem has a one-dimensional
gauge invariance (adding a constant to all entries of
\theta
leaves

\pi_{\theta}
unchanged), which we factor out by working on

\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}
modulo
\mathbf{1}
, equivalently on the subspace

\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
. We use
\|\cdot\|
for the Euclidean norm on this
quotient, which equals the Euclidean norm of any representative with
zero sum.

For a fixed preference operator
P
, the population risk-adjusted equilibrium

\theta^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}(P)
is the unique solution of the equation

0\;=\;F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta)\;:=\;\beta(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}})-P_{\mathcal{R}}\,\pi_{\theta},

(249)

viewed as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}/\mathbf{1}
. This is a

generalized equation
in the sense of Robinson (cf.
17
;
48
) with
zero set-valued component:

0\;\in\;F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta)+N(\theta),

(250)

where
N(\theta)=\{0\}
since the constraint set is the whole space
(modulo gauge). We retain the formulation (
250
) since
the analysis below transports verbatim to settings where
N
is the
normal cone of a closed convex constraint set on
\theta
(e.g.,
simplex-constrained or trust-region-clipped parameterizations).

J.1
Structural stability of the solution mapping

Treat
P\mapsto\theta^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}(P)
as a
mapping from preference operators (a vector space) to policy parameters.
Strong metric regularity of (
250
) at the population
solution amounts to local single-valuedness and Lipschitz dependence of
this solution mapping. We establish this directly using strong
monotonicity, which is the cleanest sufficient condition.

See
5

Proof.

The argument proceeds in three steps:
(i)
recast the problem as a
generalized equation,
(ii)
apply strong monotonicity to obtain
Lipschitz dependence of the solution on the operator,
(iii)
translate
to the explicit bound (
13
).

Step 1: Strong monotonicity of
F_{\mathcal{R},P}
.

By Theorem
2
, the operator
F_{\mathcal{R},P}
is

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}(P)
-strongly monotone on
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
with respect to a
KL-Bregman geometry. In Euclidean form, this means there exists a
constant
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\geq 2\epsilon
such that for all

\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
,

\bigl\langle F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta_{1})-F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta_{2}),\,\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\bigr\rangle\;\geq\;\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\,\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2}.

(251)

Step 2: Generalized equation analysis.

The two RQREs satisfy

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\;=\;F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta^{\star})\quad\text{and}\quad 0\;=\;F_{\mathcal{R},P^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime\star}).

(252)

Decompose the difference as follows:

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta^{\prime\star})-F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta^{\star})

\displaystyle\;=\;F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta^{\prime\star})-0\;=\;F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta^{\prime\star})-F_{\mathcal{R},P^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime\star})

(253)

\displaystyle\;=\;-(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\,\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}},

(254)

where the last equality uses that
F_{\mathcal{R},P}
and
F_{\mathcal{R},P^{\prime}}

differ only in their dependence on the preference operator:

F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta)-F_{\mathcal{R},P^{\prime}}(\theta)=-(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\pi_{\theta}
.

Step 3: Apply strong monotonicity.

Take inner product of (
254
) with
\theta^{\prime\star}-\theta^{\star}
to get that

\displaystyle\bigl\langle F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta^{\prime\star})-F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta^{\star}),\,\theta^{\prime\star}-\theta^{\star}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\;=\;-\bigl\langle(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\,\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}},\,\theta^{\prime\star}-\theta^{\star}\bigr\rangle.

The left-hand side is bounded below by
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\,\|\theta^{\star}-\theta^{\prime\star}\|^{2}

by (
251
). The right-hand side is bounded above (in
absolute value) by Cauchy–Schwarz—indeed,

\bigl|\bigl\langle(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}},\,\theta^{\prime\star}-\theta^{\star}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\;\leq\;\bigl\|(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}}\bigr\|\;\|\theta^{\prime\star}-\theta^{\star}\|.

Combining the two bounds we have that

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\,\|\theta^{\star}-\theta^{\prime\star}\|^{2}\;\leq\;\bigl\|(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}}\bigr\|\;\|\theta^{\prime\star}-\theta^{\star}\|,

and dividing by
\|\theta^{\prime\star}-\theta^{\star}\|
(assuming it is nonzero;
otherwise the bound is trivial) yields (
13
). The
operator-norm form follows from

\|(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}}\|\leq\|P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\|_{\rm op}\,\|\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}}\|\leq\|P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\|_{\rm op}

since
\|\pi\|_{2}\leq\|\pi\|_{1}=1
.
∎

Connection to strong metric regularity.

Theorem
5

is a special case of the general
strong metric regularity
property
of solution mappings of strongly monotone variational inequalities.
Specifically, the canonical perturbation of the generalized equation
(
250
),

v\;\in\;F_{\mathcal{R},P}(\theta)+N(\theta),\quad v\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|},

has solution mapping
S_{P}:v\mapsto\theta_{v}
that is single-valued
and globally Lipschitz with constant
1/\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
on its domain

(
16
, Theorem 3F.4)
. The bound
(
13
) corresponds to perturbing the constant term by

v=-(P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}})\pi_{\theta^{\prime\star}}
and applying this Lipschitz
constant. In the present softmax-parameterized problem the constraint
set
C
is trivial (
N\equiv\{0\}
), but the framework extends without
modification to (i) projected gradient dynamics on the simplex
(
N(\pi)=N_{\Delta(\mathcal{Y})}(\pi)
), (ii) trust-region-clipped policies
(box constraints on
\theta
), and (iii) other constrained
parameterizations one might use in practice.

Deriving Proposition
2
from Theorem
5
.

We now show that the proposition from the main is a direct consequence of Theorem
5
. For ease of reference we recall the proposition here.

See
2

Corollary 12
.

Proposition
2
is a direct consequence of
Corollary
13
(and hence of Theorem
5
), under
the identification

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}\equiv\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}

and the norm comparison
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
on

\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
.

Derivation of Proposition
2
from Theorem
5
.

We proceed in three steps: (i) apply the parameter-space stability bound,
(ii) relate the KL divergence between the RQRE policies to the parameter
distance via strong convexity of the entropy-regularized objective, and
(iii) combine the two estimates.

Step 1: Parameter-space stability.

By Theorem
5
applied symmetrically to both operators, we have

\bigl\|\theta^{\star}-\theta^{\prime\star}\bigr\|\;\leq\;\frac{\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}}.

(255)

Using the standard inequality

\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
on
\Delta(\mathcal{Y})

(since probability vectors have
\ell_{1}
-norm one), we may replace the
operator norm by the sup-norm:

\bigl\|\theta^{\star}-\theta^{\prime\star}\bigr\|\;\leq\;\frac{\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\|_{\infty}}{\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}}.

(256)

Step 2: From parameter distance to KL divergence.

The RQRE policies
\pi^{\star},\pi^{\prime\star}
arise as the unique maximizers of
strongly concave, entropy-regularized objectives
F,F^{\prime}
with curvature
moduli
\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}
, respectively. By strong concavity of
F^{\prime}

at its maximizer
\pi^{\prime\star}
,

F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime\star})-F^{\prime}(\pi^{\star})\;\geq\;\frac{\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}}{2}\,\bigl\|\theta^{\star}-\theta^{\prime\star}\bigr\|^{2}.

On the other hand, for entropy-regularized objectives the suboptimality
gap coincides (up to the regularization strength) with the KL divergence
to the optimum:

F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime\star})-F^{\prime}(\pi^{\star})\;=\;\beta\,\KL(\pi^{\star}\,\|\,\pi^{\prime\star}),

where
\beta
is the entropy-regularization parameter. Combining the two
displays and absorbing constants into the curvature modulus yields

\KL(\pi^{\star}\,\|\,\pi^{\prime\star})\;\leq\;\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}\cdot\bigl\|\theta^{\star}-\theta^{\prime\star}\bigr\|^{2}.

(257)

Step 3: Combine the bounds.

Squaring (
256
) and substituting into
(
257
),

\KL(\pi^{\star}\,\|\,\pi^{\prime\star})\;\leq\;\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}\cdot\frac{\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}^{2}}\;=\;\frac{\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}}.

A more careful accounting of the constants in Step 2 (in particular,
tracking the factor of
1/2
from strong concavity and the precise
relationship between
F^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime\star})-F^{\prime}(\pi^{\star})
and the KL) yields
the stated bound

\KL(\pi^{\star}\,\|\,\pi^{\prime\star})\;\leq\;\frac{\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\}^{2}}.\qed

Connection to Algorithmic Stability.

We can immediately use the Lipschitz continuity of the risk-adjusted equilibrium solution map to obtain algorithmic stability in the online case. For any dataset
S\sim\mathcal{D}^{n}
let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}
be the risk adjusted payoffs of a preference model trained on that dataset.

Assumption 3
.

There exists
\zeta
so that for any datasets
S,S^{\prime}
that differ in one element

\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}\leq\zeta

Typically, for a dataset of size
n
, one expects
\zeta=O(1/n)
for a stable risk minimizer, or
\zeta=O(n^{-1/2})
under concentration alone.

Theorem 17
.

Let
\pi_{S}^{\star}
and
\pi_{S^{\prime}}^{\star}
be solutions to the RQRE’s defined by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S^{\prime}}
. Under Assumption
3
and the assumptions of Theorem
4
, the bound holds:

\KL(\pi_{S}^{\star}\|\pi_{S^{\prime}}^{\star})\leq\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\min\{\mu_{\mathcal{R}},\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\}^{2}}

This is shown by applying Theorem
5
with
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S^{\prime}}
, along with Assumption
3
. Note that the uniqueness and Lipschitz continuity of the RQE are crucial to this proof.

Translating to KL via Pinsker.

For the offline statistical
result we want a
\KL
-bound on the policies, not a Euclidean bound on
parameters. By a standard softmax-Lipschitz argument, the map

\theta\mapsto\pi_{\theta}=\mathrm{softmax}(\theta)
satisfies

\KL(\pi_{\theta_{1}}\|\pi_{\theta_{2}})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2}

on bounded domains
(
8
, Lemma 13)
. Combining this with Theorem
5
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 13

(KL-stability of RQRE)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5
, where

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}=\beta-2\max\{\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{P}),\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\}

denotes the (worst-case) strong-monotonicity modulus across the two operators, the bound holds:

\KL(\pi^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}(P)\|\pi^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}(P^{\prime}))\;\leq\;\frac{1}{2\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\,\bigl\|P_{\mathcal{R}}-P^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}\bigr\|_{\rm op}^{2}.

(258)

This is the form that plugs directly into the offline sample complexity
proof (Section
K.4
).

J.2
From stability to generalization under the risk-adjusted IPO loss

We instantiate the stability-to-generalization conversion with the
risk-adjusted IPO loss, which is the natural deployment loss in our
setting. For a comparison example
z=(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
drawn from

\rho\otimes\mu
, define

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi;z)\;:=\;\left(\log\frac{\pi(y)\,\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime})}{\pi(y^{\prime})\,\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)}\;-\;\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime}\succ y^{\prime\prime})\bigr)\right)^{2}.

(259)

Taking expectations recovers the population IPO objective

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\theta;\rho,\mu)=\E_{z}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi_{\theta};z)]
from
(
26
). The empirical counterpart on a sample

S=\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\theta)=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi_{\theta};z_{i})
.

Assumption 4

(Bounded softmax mass)
.

There exists
p_{\min}>0
such that for every
\theta
in the relevant
domain (in particular,
\theta=\theta^{\star}_{S}
for any sample
S
in
the support of
\mathcal{D}^{n}
), the policy
\pi_{\theta}=\operatorname{softmax}(\theta)

satisfies
\pi_{\theta}(y)\geq p_{\min}
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

This is mild: under the QRE regularizer
\beta(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
the optimum
\theta^{\star}
lies in a bounded set determined by
\beta

and the operator norm of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}
, which yields a deterministic

p_{\min}
depending on those constants.

Lemma 19

(Explicit
p_{\min}
from the risk-adjusted equilibrium optimality condition)
.

Suppose the risk-adjusted preferences are bounded:

|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})|\leq M
for all
y,y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Suppose
further that the reference parameter satisfies

\mathrm{diam}(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}):=\max_{y}\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y)-\min_{y}\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y)\leq D_{\mathrm{ref}}
. Then for every preference operator
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}
in the
support of
\mathcal{D}^{n}
, the risk-adjusted equilibrium solution
\theta^{\star}_{S}=\theta^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S})

satisfies, after fixing the gauge so that
\sum_{y}\theta^{\star}_{S}(y)=\sum_{y}\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y)
,

\mathrm{diam}(\theta^{\star}_{S})\;\leq\;D_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{2M}{\beta}
,
and consequently

\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{S}}(y)\;\geq\;p_{\min}\;:=\;\frac{\exp\bigl(-D_{\mathrm{ref}}-2M/\beta\bigr)}{|\mathcal{Y}|}\qquad\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}.

Proof.

The RQRE optimality condition (
267
) gives

\beta(\theta^{\star}_{S}-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}})=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}\,\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{S}}
,
i.e.,

\theta^{\star}_{S}(y)-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y)\;=\;\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}\,\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{S}}\bigr)(y)\;=\;\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{y^{\prime}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}(y\succ y^{\prime})\,\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{S}}(y^{\prime}).

Since
\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{S}}
is a probability distribution and

|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}(y\succ y^{\prime})|\leq M
uniformly,

\bigl|\theta^{\star}_{S}(y)-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y)\bigr|\;\leq\;\frac{M}{\beta}\qquad\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}.

Therefore, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
,

\theta^{\star}_{S}(y_{1})-\theta^{\star}_{S}(y_{2})\;=\;\bigl[\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y_{1})-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y_{2})\bigr]+\bigl[\theta^{\star}_{S}(y_{1})-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y_{1})\bigr]-\bigl[\theta^{\star}_{S}(y_{2})-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}(y_{2})\bigr],

and taking absolute values via the triangle inequality,

\mathrm{diam}(\theta^{\star}_{S})\;\leq\;\mathrm{diam}(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}})+\frac{2M}{\beta}\;\leq\;D_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{2M}{\beta}.

For the softmax lower bound, observe that for any
y
, the sequence of lower bounds hold:

\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{S}}(y)\;=\;\frac{e^{\theta^{\star}_{S}(y)}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}}e^{\theta^{\star}_{S}(y^{\prime})}}\;\geq\;\frac{e^{\theta^{\star}_{S}(y)}}{|\mathcal{Y}|\,e^{\max_{y^{\prime}}\theta^{\star}_{S}(y^{\prime})}}\;=\;\frac{e^{-(\max_{y^{\prime}}\theta^{\star}_{S}(y^{\prime})-\theta^{\star}_{S}(y))}}{|\mathcal{Y}|}\;\geq\;\frac{e^{-\mathrm{diam}(\theta^{\star}_{S})}}{|\mathcal{Y}|}.

Combining these observations yields the claim.
∎

Regularity of the IPO Loss.

Now we prove Lipschitz continuity of the risk-adjusted IPO loss in
\pi
.

Lemma 20

(Lipschitzness of the risk-adjusted IPO loss in
\pi
)
.

Suppose Assumption
4
holds and additionally

\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)\geq p_{\min}
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
. Suppose further that
the risk-adjusted preferences are bounded:

|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})|\leq M
for all
y,y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
.
Then the loss (
259
) satisfies, for any

\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\{q\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y}):q(y)\geq p_{\min}\ \forall y\}

and any comparison example
z=(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
,

\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi;z)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\prime};z)\bigr|\;\leq\;L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}\,\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}},\qquad L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}\;:=\;\frac{8D}{p_{\min}},

where

D:=2\log(1/p_{\min})+2M/\beta

is an upper bound on the absolute value of the residual inside the
square in (
259
).

Proof.

Define the residual

r(\pi;z)\;:=\;\log\frac{\pi(y)\,\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime})}{\pi(y^{\prime})\,\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)}\;-\;\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime}\succ y^{\prime\prime})\bigr),

so that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi;z)=r(\pi;z)^{2}
. Split the residual into its

\pi
-dependent and
\pi
-independent parts:

r(\pi;z)\;=\;\underbrace{\log\pi(y)-\log\pi(y^{\prime})}_{=:\,a(\pi;z)}\;+\;\underbrace{\log\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime})-\log\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)-\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime}\succ y^{\prime\prime})\bigr)}_{=:\,c(z)}.

We proceed in three steps: bound
|r(\pi;z)|
uniformly, establish
Lipschitzness of
a(\pi;z)
in
\pi
, and combine via the
chain rule for the squared loss.

Step 1: Uniform bound on
|r(\pi;z)|
.

Since
\pi(y),\pi(y^{\prime})\geq p_{\min}
and
\pi(y),\pi(y^{\prime})\leq 1
,

|a(\pi;z)|\;=\;|\log\pi(y)-\log\pi(y^{\prime})|\;\leq\;2\log(1/p_{\min}).

By the same argument applied to
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}
,

|\log\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y^{\prime})-\log\pi_{\texttt{ref}}(y)|\leq 2\log(1/p_{\min})
. The
preference term is bounded by

\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime}\succ y^{\prime\prime})\bigr|\;\leq\;\frac{2M}{\beta}.

Adding these contributions, however, would double-count:
r
contains
the
\pi
-dependent log-ratio
a(\pi;z)
once and the

\pi
-independent terms once. The triangle inequality gives

|r(\pi;z)|\;\leq\;|a(\pi;z)|+|c(z)|\;\leq\;2\log(1/p_{\min})+2\log(1/p_{\min})+\frac{2M}{\beta}\;\leq\;D,

with
D:=4\log(1/p_{\min})+2M/\beta
. (We absorb the factor of two
into
D
rather than the Lipschitz constant for cleanliness; the precise
form of
D
is unimportant for the qualitative result.)

Step 2: Lipschitzness of
a(\pi;z)
in
\pi
.

Fix
z=(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
and consider
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
in the admissible set.
It is immediate that

a(\pi;z)-a(\pi^{\prime};z)\;=\;\bigl(\log\pi(y)-\log\pi^{\prime}(y)\bigr)-\bigl(\log\pi(y^{\prime})-\log\pi^{\prime}(y^{\prime})\bigr).

The function
t\mapsto\log t
is
1/p_{\min}
-Lipschitz on

[p_{\min},1]
, since its derivative
1/t
is bounded by
1/p_{\min}

on this interval. Therefore, for any single coordinate
u\in\{y,y^{\prime}\}
, we have

|\log\pi(u)-\log\pi^{\prime}(u)|\;\leq\;\frac{1}{p_{\min}}|\pi(u)-\pi^{\prime}(u)|\;\leq\;\frac{1}{p_{\min}}\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\infty}.

Applying the triangle inequality it is immediate that

|a(\pi;z)-a(\pi^{\prime};z)|\;\leq\;\frac{2}{p_{\min}}\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\infty}.

Since
\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{1}=2\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
,
this yields

|a(\pi;z)-a(\pi^{\prime};z)|\;\leq\;\frac{4}{p_{\min}}\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Since
c(z)
does not depend on
\pi
, we obtain the same Lipschitz continuity
bound for
r
—indeed, we have

|r(\pi;z)-r(\pi^{\prime};z)|\;\leq\;\frac{4}{p_{\min}}\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

(260)

Step 3: Lipschitzness of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}=r^{2}
.

For any reals
u,v
with
|u|,|v|\leq D
, it is immediate that

|u^{2}-v^{2}|\;=\;|u+v|\cdot|u-v|\;\leq\;2D\,|u-v|.

Applying this to
u=r(\pi;z)
and
v=r(\pi^{\prime};z)
, both bounded in
absolute value by
D
from Step 1, we have that

|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi;z)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\prime};z)|\;=\;|r(\pi;z)^{2}-r(\pi^{\prime};z)^{2}|\;\leq\;2D\,|r(\pi;z)-r(\pi^{\prime};z)|.

Combining this bound with (
260
) yields

|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi;z)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\prime};z)|\;\leq\;2D\cdot\frac{4}{p_{\min}}\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\;=\;\frac{8D}{p_{\min}}\,\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Setting
L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}:=8D/p_{\min}
completes the proof.
∎

J.3
Algorithmic Stability Generalization Bounds in Expectation

We now convert the algorithmic stability of
Theorem
17
into a generalization
guarantee under the risk-adjusted IPO loss. The argument requires
two ingredients beyond stability: a bounded admissible policy class
on which the IPO loss is Lipschitz, and a Lipschitzness lemma for
the loss itself. We state these in turn before the main theorem.

Assumption 5

(Bounded admissible policy class)
.

There exists a class
\Pi\subseteq\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
such that:

i.

\pi^{\star}_{S}\in\Pi
almost surely for
S\sim\mathcal{D}^{n}
, and
\pi_{\texttt{ref}}\in\Pi
;

ii.

there exists
L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}<\infty
such that for all
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi
and all comparison examples
z=(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
,

|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi;z)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\prime};z)|\;\leq\;L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}\,\|\pi-\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}};

iii.

there exists
B<\infty
such that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi;z)\leq B
for all
\pi\in\Pi
and all
z
.

Remark 3

(Sufficient conditions)
.

Assumption
5
is satisfied whenever the admissible
policies are bounded away from zero, i.e.
\pi(y)\geq p_{\min}
for all

y
and all
\pi\in\Pi
, and the risk-adjusted preferences satisfy

|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(y\succ y^{\prime\prime})|\leq M
. In that case

L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}=8D/p_{\min}
and
B=D^{2}
, with

D:=4\log(1/p_{\min})+2M/\beta
, by Lemma
20
.
Such a uniform
p_{\min}
exists, for example, on any bounded set in

\theta
-space, which is in turn implied by the RQRE optimality
condition
\beta(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{ref}})=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\pi_{\theta}
together
with boundedness of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}
(see Lemma
19
).

Theorem 18

(Stability-based generalization of risk-adjusted equilibrium)
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
17

hold, together with Assumption
5
. Let

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{\min}
be a uniform lower bound on the strong-monotonicity
modulus over the support of
\mathcal{D}^{n}
, and let
\zeta
be the
preference-model stability constant of Assumption
3
.
Then the risk-adjusted equilibrium policy
\pi^{\star}_{S}
satisfies

\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{S}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\bigr]\Bigr|\;\leq\;\beta_{n},\qquad\beta_{n}\;:=\;L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Y}|}\,\zeta}{\sqrt{2}\,\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{\min}}.

In particular, with
\zeta=O(1/n)
for a stable preference-model
fitter, the expected IPO generalization gap is
O(1/n)
; with

\zeta=O(n^{-1/2})
, it is
O(n^{-1/2})
.

Proof.

The argument proceeds in three steps:
(i)
establish uniform stability
of the map
S\mapsto\pi^{\star}_{S}
with respect to the IPO loss,

(ii)
apply the replace-one symmetrization identity, and
(iii)
bound
the resulting expectation.

Step 1: Uniform stability with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}
.

Let

S=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\quad\text{ and }\quad S^{(i)}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},z_{i}^{\prime},z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{n})

denote
two samples differing only in their
i
-th element. By
Theorem
17
, we have the upper bound

\KL\bigl(\pi^{\star}_{S}\,\bigl\|\,\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}}\bigr)\;\leq\;\frac{\zeta^{2}\cdot|\mathcal{Y}|}{(\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{\min})^{2}},

where the factor
|\mathcal{Y}|
converts the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
bound on
preference operators (Assumption
3
) to the operator
norm appearing in Theorem
5
, via

\|P\|_{\rm op}\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{Y}|}\,\|P\|_{\infty}
. Pinsker’s
inequality then gives

\|\pi^{\star}_{S}-\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\;\leq\;\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}\KL(\pi^{\star}_{S}\,\|\,\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}})}\;\leq\;\frac{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Y}|}\,\zeta}{\sqrt{2}\,\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{\min}}.

By Assumption
5
(ii), for any comparison example

z
, we have the bound

\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};z)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z)\bigr|\;\leq\;L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Y}|}\,\zeta}{\sqrt{2}\,\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{\min}}\;=\;\beta_{n}.

(261)

This is uniform stability of the RQRE map with parameter
\beta_{n}
.

Step 2: Replace-one symmetrization.

Let
S=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
and

\widetilde{S}=(\widetilde{z}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{n})
be two
independent i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{D}^{n}
, and for each

i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
define

S^{(i)}:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},\widetilde{z}_{i},z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{n})
.
The population and empirical risks satisfy

\mathbb{E}_{S}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\bigr]\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{S,\widetilde{z}}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};\widetilde{z})\bigr],\qquad\mathbb{E}_{S}\bigl[\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\bigr]\;=\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{S}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};z_{i})\bigr].

Since
\widetilde{z}
in the first expression is i.i.d. from
\mathcal{D}

and independent of
S
, we may rename it
\widetilde{z}_{i}
for any
i

without changing the expectation—that is,

\mathbb{E}_{S}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\bigr]\;=\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\E_{S,\widetilde{z}_{i}}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};\widetilde{z}_{i})\bigr].

The pair
(S,\widetilde{z}_{i})
has the same joint distribution as

(S^{(i)},z_{i})
—i.e., both consist of
n+1
i.i.d. draws with one
designated as the “held-out” point—so by relabeling,

\mathbb{E}_{S,\widetilde{z}_{i}}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};\widetilde{z}_{i})\bigr]\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{S,\widetilde{z}_{i}}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z_{i})\bigr].

Combining these observations yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{S}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\bigr]

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{S,\widetilde{z}_{i}}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z_{i})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};z_{i})\bigr].

(262)

Step 3: Apply uniform stability.

The samples
S
and
S^{(i)}
differ only in their
i
-th coordinate
(
z_{i}
vs.
\widetilde{z}_{i}
), so by (
261
)
evaluated on
z=z_{i}
, we have that

\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z_{i})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};z_{i})\bigr|\;\leq\;\beta_{n}\quad\text{a.s.}

Taking expectations and applying the triangle inequality to
(
262
) yields

\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{S}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\bigr]\Bigr|\;\leq\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{S,\widetilde{z}_{i}}\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z_{i})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S};z_{i})\bigr|\;\leq\;\beta_{n}.\qed

Remark 4

(Explicit constants in the IPO loss bounds)
.

Under the boundedness assumption
|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}|\leq M
and

\mathrm{diam}(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}})\leq D_{\mathrm{ref}}
, Lemma
19

gives

p_{\min}\;=\;\frac{e^{-(D_{\mathrm{ref}}+2M/\beta)}}{|\mathcal{Y}|}.

Substituting this into Lemma
20
yields

L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}\;=\;\frac{8D}{p_{\min}}\;=\;8D\,|\mathcal{Y}|\,e^{D_{\mathrm{ref}}+2M/\beta},\qquad\text{where}\;\;B\;=\;D^{2},

and
D=4\log(1/p_{\min})+2M/\beta=4\bigl(\log|\mathcal{Y}|+D_{\mathrm{ref}}+2M/\beta\bigr)+2M/\beta
.
The dependence on
\beta
is exponential through the factor

e^{2M/\beta}
, which is unsurprising: small regularization
\beta\to 0

allows the RQRE policy to concentrate arbitrarily, blowing up the
log-ratio in the IPO loss. The dependence on
|\mathcal{Y}|
is linear
through the explicit factor and logarithmic through
D
.

J.4
Algorithmic Stability High Probability Generalization Bounds

Theorem
18
bounds the generalization gap
in expectation. For a high-probability bound, the standard route is to
view
\Phi(S):=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})

as a function of the i.i.d. sample
S
and apply a concentration
inequality exploiting bounded differences. The classical

5
approach uses McDiarmid’s inequality, with
the difference constant inherited from uniform stability:

|\Phi(S)-\Phi(S^{(i)})|\leq 2\beta_{n}+B/n
. This is informative
only when
\beta_{n}=O(1/n)
, which in our setting requires the
preference-model stability constant
\zeta
from
Assumption
3
to scale as
\zeta=O(1/n)
.

This fast-stability regime is unrealistic in our setting. The

\zeta=O(1/n)
rate is characteristic of preference fitters that are
themselves uniformly stable algorithms—for instance, ERM with a
strongly convex regularizer. Although the risk-adjusted equilibrium objective itself is
KL-regularized at strength
\beta
, that regularization controls how
the optimal policy
\theta^{\star}
responds to a given preference
operator, not how the preference operator
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R},S}
depends on
the training sample. These two stabilities are decoupled:
\beta

governs the operator-to-policy map (the
1/\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
constant of
Theorem
5
), while
\zeta
governs the sample-to-operator
map, which is a property of the preference fitter itself. In typical
modern RLHF pipelines, where the preference model is a deep network
trained by SGD on a Bradley–Terry-style objective, the best one can
generically argue is the concentration rate

\zeta=O(n^{-1/2})
. Moreover, even if a fast regime were attainable
in principle, the explicit constants of
Remark
4
scale as
e^{2M/\beta}
, so one
cannot tune
\beta
to reach the fast regime without paying an
exponential price in the loss-Lipschitz constant.

The slow-stability regime
\zeta=O(n^{-1/2})
is therefore the one
that matters in practice, and McDiarmid’s inequality is inadequate
there: the term
n\beta_{n}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}
becomes
O(1)
and
the bound carries no information. Fortunately, the gap between the
in-expectation and high-probability rates can be closed using the
sharper concentration of
6
, which exploits
the weak correlation between coordinate-wise perturbations of a
uniformly stable algorithm. Their bound replaces the prohibitive

n\beta_{n}
factor by
\beta_{n}\log n
, yielding a high-probability rate
that matches the in-expectation rate up to logarithmic factors in both
regimes. We state the resulting corollary below.

Corollary 14

(Sharp high-probability generalization bound)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
18
,
there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that for any

\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
over the
draw of
S\sim\mathcal{D}^{n}
, the estimate holds:

\Bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\Bigr|\;\leq\;c\left(\beta_{n}\log n\log\frac{1}{\delta}\;+\;B\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right),

(263)

where
\beta_{n}=L_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{Y}|}\,\zeta/(\sqrt{2}\,\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{\min})

is the uniform stability parameter from
Theorem
18
and
B
is the uniform bound
on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}
from Assumption
5
(iii).

Proof.

Define, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the expected loss gap

g_{i}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\;:=\;\E_{\widetilde{z}_{i}}\!\left[\,\E_{z}\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z)\bigr]\;-\;\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z_{i})\,\right],

where
S^{(i)}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},\widetilde{z}_{i},z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{n})

is
S
with its
i
-th coordinate replaced by an independent copy

\widetilde{z}_{i}
, and the inner expectation is over a fresh

z\sim\mathcal{D}
. We verify that the
g_{i}
satisfy the three conditions
of Theorem 4 of
6
:

i.

Conditional bound
|\E[g_{i}\mid z_{i}]|\leq M
.

By Assumption
5
(iii),
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}\leq B
uniformly,
so the inner difference is bounded in absolute value by
B
, and any
expectation thereof is at most
B
in absolute value. Thus

|\E[g_{i}\mid z_{i}]|\leq B
, so we set
M:=B
.

ii.

Centering
\E[g_{i}\mid z_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}]=0
.

Conditional on
z_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}
, the inner expectation

\E_{\widetilde{z}_{i}}\E_{z}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z)]
does not depend
on
z_{i}
(since
z_{i}
has been replaced by
\widetilde{z}_{i}
in

S^{(i)}
). The second term, when averaged over
z_{i}
, becomes

\E_{\widetilde{z}_{i},z_{i}}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z_{i})]
. Since

z_{i}
and
\widetilde{z}_{i}
are i.i.d. and the latter appears in

S^{(i)}
in place of
z_{i}
, by exchangeability this equals

\E_{\widetilde{z}_{i},z}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z)]
where

z\sim\mathcal{D}
independently. The two terms cancel, giving

\E[g_{i}\mid z_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}]=0
.

iii.

Bounded differences in coordinates
j\neq i
.

Fix
j\neq i
and consider modifying
z_{j}
to
z_{j}^{\prime}
. This changes

S^{(i)}
in coordinate
j
only. By the uniform stability bound
(
261
), replacing one coordinate of the training
set changes
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S^{(i)}};z)
by at most
\beta_{n}
for
any
z
. Therefore both terms inside the brackets change by at most

\beta_{n}
, and
g_{i}
changes by at most
2\beta_{n}
. Thus
g_{i}
has
bounded differences with parameter
\beta:=2\beta_{n}
in every
coordinate
j\neq i
.

Connecting
\sum_{i}g_{i}
to the generalization gap.

By Lemma 7 of
6
, under uniform stability
(
261
) and uniform boundedness
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}\leq B
,

\Bigl|n\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\bigr)\Bigr|\;\leq\;\Bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Bigr|\;+\;2n\beta_{n}\quad\text{a.s.}

Applying the BKZ moment bound.

By Theorem 4 of
6
, for any
p\geq 2
,

\Bigl\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Bigr\|_{p}\;\leq\;12\sqrt{2}\,p\,n\,\beta_{n}\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil\;+\;4B\sqrt{pn}.

Lemma 1 of
6
converts this moment bound into
a tail bound: with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\Bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Bigr|\;\leq\;c_{1}\,n\,\beta_{n}\log n\log\!\frac{1}{\delta}\;+\;c_{2}\,B\sqrt{n\log(1/\delta)}

for absolute constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
. Combining with the inequality
above and dividing by
n
,

\Bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{IPO}}^{\mathcal{R}}(\pi^{\star}_{S})\Bigr|\;\leq\;c_{1}\,\beta_{n}\log n\log\!\frac{1}{\delta}\;+\;c_{2}\,B\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\;+\;2\beta_{n}.

The final
2\beta_{n}
term is dominated by the first term for
n\geq 2
,

\delta\leq 1
, so absorbing all constants into a single
c>0
yields
(
263
).
∎

Appendix K
Offline sample complexity

In this section we establish a statistical guarantee for the risk-adjusted
NLHF problem in the offline setting: given
n
iid preference comparisons,
how close to the population risk-adjusted QRE

\pi^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}
can we get? The result is a fast

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/n)
rate, generalizing the recent
result of
70
for risk-neutral games. In their
risk-neutral case the rate is governed by the KL temperature
\beta
;
under risk, the rate is governed by the strong-monotonicity modulus

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}=\beta-2\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}
, and the constants pick up
explicit dependence on the Lipschitz constant of the risk dual map and
the leading bias coefficient.

K.1
Technical Novelties & Hurdles

To better understand the contributions, we first outline the technical hurdles before diving in.

Structural bias from risk.

In the risk-neutral setting, the preference operator
P
satisfies the
constant-sum identity
P+P^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
, which
yields a zero-sum game and enables a single-operator variational
inequality formulation. Under risk, the induced operator
P_{\mathcal{R}}

is nonlinear in the opponent distribution, and in general

P_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(\mu)+P_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(\mu)^{\top}\neq\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}.

As a result, the game becomes
general-sum
, and the analysis must
proceed via the joint pseudogradient rather than a single monotone operator.
There is also a
risk-distortion term
that perturbs the monotonicity
structure and must be controlled to retain strong monotonicity.

Statistical bias from risk estimation.

Unlike expectation, risk functionals are nonlinear, so Monte Carlo
estimators are inherently biased. For plug-in estimators of the form

\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta)=h(\widehat{q}_{m}(\theta))
, where

q(\theta)=\E[g(\theta,Y^{\prime\prime})]
, the bias admits a delta-method expansion

\E[\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta)]-F_{\mathcal{R}}(\theta)=\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(q(\theta))}{2m}\mathrm{Var}(g(\theta,Y^{\prime\prime}))+\mathcal{O}(m^{-3/2}).

This induces a persistent bias floor of order
\mathcal{O}(1/m)
in the optimization
error, which fundamentally limits achievable accuracy unless corrected.
Our analysis explicitly tracks this bias through the extra-gradient dynamics.

Bernstein-type concentration for nonlinear risk.

Controlling stochastic fluctuations of the risk estimator requires
new concentration arguments. Standard Hoeffding-type bounds are
insufficient due to the nonlinear transformation
h(\cdot)
.
Instead, we develop a Bernstein-style concentration bound that exploits
variance control of the inner statistic
g(\theta,Y^{\prime\prime})
together with
smoothness of
h
. This yields high-probability bounds of the form

\|\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta)-\E[\widehat{F}_{\mathcal{R},m}(\theta)]\|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Var}(g)}{m}}+\frac{1}{m},

uniformly over the iterates, which are crucial for obtaining sharp
finite-sample rates.

Connection to the rest of the paper.

Theorem
7
is the offline analog of the online
convergence guarantee for risk-adjusted extra-gradient (Proposition
1
) and the
two-timescale debiasing result (Theorem
15
). All three rely on the
same underlying object: the strong-monotonicity modulus

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}=\beta-2\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}
derived in
Theorem
2
. In the offline setting,
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
governs the
sample complexity rate. In the online setting,
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
governs the
convergence rate
1-\eta\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
of EG iterates and the bias
floor
\mathcal{O}(B_{m}/\mu_{\mathcal{R}})
. The condition

\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\beta/2-\varepsilon
is therefore the

single technical assumption
that delivers all three: fast
convergence in iterations, vanishing bias floor under two-timescale
debiasing, and fast statistical rate in offline samples. Conversely,
the threshold
\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}=\beta/2
is a fundamental boundary:
it bounds the regime in which strong monotonicity holds, and we expect
all three properties to fail simultaneously beyond it.

This unified role of
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
is the technical content of the
“aligned risk reinforces regularization” message: when
\Delta_{\mathcal{R}}\preceq 0
,

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
is strengthened beyond
\beta
, and
all three

guarantees improve simultaneously—faster convergence, smaller bias
floor, and faster statistical rate.

K.2
Preliminaries and main result

Let
\bar{\pi}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
be a fixed sampling
distribution. We observe
n
iid samples

\{(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, with
(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\sim\bar{\pi}\otimes\bar{\pi}

and
z_{i}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(P(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}))
where
P_{y,y^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime})

satisfies the constant-sum identity
P+P^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
.
We do not access the true
P
directly; only the
n
comparisons.

For the entropic risk operator

\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{\rm ent}
, define the empirical risk-adjusted operator

(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}:=-\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\left(\sum_{y^{\prime\prime}}\widehat{\mu}(y^{\prime\prime})\,\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})\right),\quad\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime}):=\frac{1}{n_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}}\sum_{i:(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})=(y,y^{\prime\prime})}\exp(-\lambda\,z_{i}),

(264)

where
\widehat{\mu}=\mu
is the (known) opponent distribution, and
n_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}=\#\{i:(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})=(y,y^{\prime\prime})\}
.
6
6

6

We treat
\mu
as known to the learner; this matches the offline-NLHF setup where the learner chooses comparison pairs but does not know
P
.

The empirical risk-adjusted QRE

\widehat{\pi}_{n}
is the unique fixed point of

\widehat{\theta}_{n}=\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}+\frac{\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\widehat{\pi}_{n}}{\beta},\qquad\widehat{\pi}_{n}=\mathrm{softmax}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}).

The following is the sample complexity result from the main paper restated for convience.

See
7

A corollary to this result is the special case where
\mathcal{R}
is entropy.

Corollary 15

(Offline sample complexity, risk-adjusted NLHF)
.

Suppose
\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\beta/2-\varepsilon
for some

\varepsilon>0
, so that
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}:=\beta-2\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\geq 2\varepsilon
.
Let
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
be the empirical RQRE defined above. For
entropic risk
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{\rm ent}
with
\lambda>0
,
there is a constant
K=K(\lambda)
such that with probability at
least
1-\delta
,

\KL(\pi^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}\|\widehat{\pi}_{n})\;\leq\;\frac{K\,e^{4\lambda}\log(|\mathcal{Y}|/\delta)}{\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}\cdot n}.

(265)

The rate is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/n)
, matching the risk-neutral rate of

70
, with constants that capture the cost of
risk:
e^{4\lambda}/\mu_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}
. As
\bar{\lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}\to\beta/2

the bound blows up, reflecting that the boundary of strong monotonicity
is also the boundary of fast statistical learning.

In the following subsections we construct both the proof of Theorem
7
and Corollary
15
. They have the same components by and large.

K.3
Proof of Theorem
7

The proof has three components, addressed in
Sections
K.3.1
–
K.3.3
, then assembled
in Section
K.4
.

K.3.1
Component 1: bias of the plug-in operator

We first quantify the deterministic bias.
For coherent risk measures admitting the Föllmer–Schied dual representation

\mathcal{R}[Z]=\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\E_{\mu}[q(Y)Z(Y)],

the empirical estimator replaces the expectation with a sample average:

(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}=\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q(Y_{i})z_{i}.

For each fixed
q
, the estimator is unbiased:

\E\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q(Y_{i})z_{i}\right]=\E[q(Y)Z(Y)].

Thus, unlike the entropic case, there is no nonlinear transformation
introducing a delta-method bias. The statistical error arises entirely
from the supremum over
q\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Lemma 21

(Maximization bias for coherent-risk plug-in estimator)
.

Fix
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
. Let

(P_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}=\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[q(Y)Z_{y}(Y)],\qquad(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}=\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q(Y_{i})Z_{y}(Y_{i}),

where
Z_{y}(Y)=P(y\succ Y)
and
0\leq q\leq M_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then

0\leq\E[(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}]-(P_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}\leq\E\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q(Y_{i})Z_{y}(Y_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[q(Y)Z_{y}(Y)]\right|.

In particular, if
\mathcal{Q}
is finite, then

\E[(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}]-(P_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}\leq CM_{\mathcal{R}}\sqrt{\frac{\log|\mathcal{Q}|}{n}}.

More generally, the right-hand side is bounded by the Rademacher
complexity of the dual class

\mathcal{F}_{y}=\{Y\mapsto q(Y)Z_{y}(Y):q\in\mathcal{Q}\}.

Proof.

The lower bound follows from Jensen:

\E\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\widehat{L}_{n}(q)\geq\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\E\widehat{L}_{n}(q).

For the upper bound,

\E\sup_{q}\widehat{L}_{n}(q)-\sup_{q}L(q)\leq\E\sup_{q}\bigl(\widehat{L}_{n}(q)-L(q)\bigr)\leq\E\sup_{q}|\widehat{L}_{n}(q)-L(q)|.

The finite-class bound follows from symmetrization and Massart’s lemma,
using
0\leq q(Y)Z_{y}(Y)\leq M_{\mathcal{R}}
.
∎

For the entropic case, we have more structure.

Lemma 22

(Plug-in bias for entropic risk)
.

For entropic risk
\mathcal{R}^{\lambda}_{\rm ent}
and any
\mu
supported on
\mathcal{Y}
,
the plug-in estimator
\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu
from
n
samples satisfies

\bigl\|\E[\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu]-P_{\mathcal{R}}\mu\bigr\|_{\infty}\;\leq\;\frac{e^{2\lambda}}{2\lambda\,n_{\min}},

where
n_{\min}=\min_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}\,\#\{i:(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})=(y,y^{\prime\prime})\}
.

Proof.

Fix any
y
, and write
g(y,y^{\prime\prime}):=\exp(-\lambda P(y,y^{\prime\prime}))
so that

(P_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}=-\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\log\left(\sum_{y^{\prime\prime}}\mu(y^{\prime\prime})g(y,y^{\prime\prime})\right).

The plug-in is the same expression with
g(y,y^{\prime\prime})
replaced by

\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})=\frac{1}{n_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}}\sum_{i}\exp(-\lambda z_{i})
.
Note that
\E[\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})]=\E[\exp(-\lambda z)]=(1-P_{y,y^{\prime\prime}})+P_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}\,e^{-\lambda}\neq g(y,y^{\prime\prime})
in general.
However, by the bias-correction identity for entropic risk

(
32
, Lemma 3.2)
, the leading-order bias of the
plug-in is exactly the delta-method term:

\displaystyle\E\left[-\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\left(\sum_{y^{\prime\prime}}\mu(y^{\prime\prime})\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})\right)\right]-\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\left(\sum_{y^{\prime\prime}}\mu(y^{\prime\prime})\E[\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})]\right)\right)

(266)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}(\widehat{g}(y,\cdot))}{2\lambda\cdot\E_{\mu}[\widehat{g}(y,\cdot)]^{2}\cdot n_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}}+R,

where the residual
R
satisfies
|R|\leq e^{4\lambda}/(\lambda\,n_{\min}^{2})

by uniform boundedness of the third derivative of
-\frac{1}{\lambda}\log(\cdot)

on
[e^{-\lambda},1]
.

The leading term is bounded:

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}(\widehat{g})\leq 1
,
\E_{\mu}[\widehat{g}]\geq e^{-\lambda}
,
so the leading term is at most
e^{2\lambda}/(2\lambda\,n_{\min})
.
The second sentence in the bias is similar. Combining,

|\E[(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}]-(P_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}|\leq\frac{e^{2\lambda}}{2\lambda\,n_{\min}}+\mathcal{O}(e^{4\lambda}/n_{\min}^{2}),

giving the claim once
n_{\min}
is large.
∎

Remark 5
.

For sampling from
\bar{\pi}
uniform with sample size
n
,
n_{\min}\asymp n/|\mathcal{Y}|^{2}

in expectation, so the per-entry bias becomes
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Y}|^{2}e^{2\lambda}/(\lambda n))
.

K.3.2
Component 2: concentration of the plug-in operator

We now control the deviation of
\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu
from its mean.
The crucial point: even though the plug-in is biased, it concentrates
around its mean at a Bernstein-type rate.

Lemma 23

(Concentration of plug-in entropic operator)
.

For each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the estimate holds:

\bigl|(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}-\E[(\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu)_{y}]\bigr|\;\leq\;\frac{e^{2\lambda}}{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2/\delta)}{n_{\min}}}.

Further, by a union bound over
y
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the estimate holds:

\|\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu-\E[\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{R}}\mu]\|_{\infty}\;\leq\;\frac{e^{2\lambda}}{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2|\mathcal{Y}|/\delta)}{n_{\min}}}.

Proof.

Fix any
y
. Let
h(\widehat{g}):=-\frac{1}{\lambda}\log(\sum_{y^{\prime\prime}}\mu(y^{\prime\prime})\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime}))
.
The function
h
is Lipschitz in
\widehat{g}
on the domain

\widehat{g}\in[e^{-\lambda},1]^{|\mathcal{Y}|}
with Lipschitz constant

L_{h}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda\cdot\min_{y}\E_{\mu}[\widehat{g}(y,\cdot)]}\leq\frac{e^{\lambda}}{\lambda}

since
\sum_{y^{\prime\prime}}\mu(y^{\prime\prime})\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})\geq\min_{y^{\prime\prime}}\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})\geq e^{-\lambda}
.
Each entry
\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})
is an average of
n_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}
i.i.d. bounded
random variables in
[e^{-\lambda},1]
, with range
1-e^{-\lambda}\leq 1
.
By Höeffding, we have that

\Pr\left(|\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})-\E\widehat{g}(y,y^{\prime\prime})|\geq t\right)\leq 2e^{-2n_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}t^{2}}.

By the union bound over
y^{\prime\prime}
and Lipschitz composition, we have that
